Part: Models and bounds (85)

1. Integer linear programming (33)

pli-f-001 · level facile · notes
Let $P = \{\vet x \in \R^n : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet x \ge \vet 0\}$. What is the feasible region $F$ of the ILP problem with the same data?
  1. $F = P \cap \Z^n$
  2. $F = P$
  3. $F = \mathrm{conv}(P \cap \Z^n)$
  4. $F = \Z^n_{\ge 0}$
Explanation. The feasible region is the set of the points of the integer lattice that lie in $P$; the polyhedron $P$ is the feasible region of the LP relaxation.
pli-f-002 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region (the set of feasible integer solutions) of an ILP problem with at least two feasible solutions. What can be said about the convexity of $F$?
  1. It is convex, because it is the intersection of the polyhedron $P$ with the lattice, both convex.
  2. In general it is not convex: the midpoint of two feasible solutions can be non-integer.
  3. It is convex if and only if the polyhedron $P$ is bounded, because then $F$ is finite.
  4. It is always convex, because every finite set of integer points is convex.
Explanation. $F$ is made of isolated points of the lattice: it is not convex. The lattice $\Z^n$ is not a convex set, so $P \cap \Z^n$ is not an intersection of convex sets.
pli-f-003 · level facile · notes
What is a linear programming problem called in which only some of the variables must be integer?
  1. A BIP problem
  2. A pure ILP problem
  3. A MIP problem
  4. An LP relaxation
Explanation. If only some variables are integer the problem is mixed (MIP); if all the variables are in $\{0,1\}$ it is a BIP problem.
pli-f-004 · level facile · notes
Consider an ILP problem $\max\{\vet c\T \vet x : \vet x \in F\}$, with feasible region $F = P \cap \Z^n$ and polyhedron $P = \{\vet x \in \R^n : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet x \ge \vet 0\}$. The polyhedron $P$ is bounded and $F \neq \emptyset$. What can be concluded?
  1. The problem can be unbounded, because $F$ is not convex.
  2. $F$ can contain infinitely many points, but the maximum exists.
  3. It has an optimal solution only if the LP relaxation has an integer optimum.
  4. $F$ is a finite set and the problem has an optimal solution.
Explanation. A bounded polyhedron contains finitely many integer points; the maximum of a function over a non-empty finite set exists.
pli-f-005 · level facile · notes
What can be said about the problem $\max\{x_1 : 2x_1 \le 1,\ -2x_1 \le -1,\ x_1 \in \Z_{\ge 0}\}$?
  1. It is infeasible, although the LP problem with the same data is feasible.
  2. Its optimal value is $\frac{1}{2}$, attained at the only feasible point.
  3. Its optimal value is $0$, attained by rounding $x_1 = \frac{1}{2}$ down.
  4. It is unbounded, because the constraint $-2x_1 \le -1$ does not bound $x_1$ from above.
Explanation. The constraints require $x_1 = \frac{1}{2}$, which is not integer: no integer point is feasible, while the LP problem has the unique solution $x_1 = \frac{1}{2}$.
pli-f-006 · level facile · notes
Complete enumeration of a problem with $n$ binary variables examines all the $2^n$ solutions. With a computer $1000$ times faster, in the same time, how many more binary variables can be enumerated?
  1. Twice as many variables
  2. About $10$ variables
  3. About $1000$ variables
  4. About $100$ variables
Explanation. Since $2^{10} = 1024 \approx 1000$, a factor $1000$ in speed compensates only about $10$ more variables.
pli-f-007 · level facile · notes
How many solutions do $n$ binary variables without other constraints have?
  1. $n^2$
  2. $2n$
  3. $2^n$
  4. $n!$
Explanation. Each variable is $0$ or $1$ independently of the others: the solutions are the $2^n$ vertices of the $n$-dimensional hypercube.
pli-f-008 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$), which of the following points does <u>not</u> belong to the feasible region $F$?
  1. $(2,2)$
  2. $(4,1)$
  3. $(0,3)$
  4. $(4,2)$
Explanation. $(4,2)$ violates the first constraint: $10 \cdot 4 + 4 \cdot 2 = 48 > 45$. The other three points satisfy both constraints.
pli-f-009 · level media · notes
Consider the ILP problem $\max\{x_1 + x_2 : 2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 7,\ x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}\}$ and let $z(\ILP)$ be its optimal value. What are $z(\ILP)$ and the number of optimal solutions?
  1. $z(\ILP) = 3$, with $4$ optimal solutions
  2. $z(\ILP) = \frac{7}{2}$, with a unique optimal solution
  3. $z(\ILP) = 3$, with the unique optimal solution $(3,0)$
  4. $z(\ILP) = 3$, with $3$ optimal solutions
Explanation. For integer points $x_1 + x_2 \le \frac{7}{2}$ implies $x_1 + x_2 \le 3$; the value $3$ is attained at $(0,3)$, $(1,2)$, $(2,1)$ and $(3,0)$. The value $\frac{7}{2}$ is the one of the LP relaxation.
pli-f-010 · level teorema · notes
Consider an ILP problem $\max\{\vet c\T \vet x : \vet x \in F\}$, with feasible region $F = P \cap \Z^n$ and polyhedron $P = \{\vet x \in \R^n : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet x \ge \vet 0\}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. If the polyhedron $P$ is unbounded and $F \neq \emptyset$, the problem can still have an optimal solution.
  2. If the LP problem with the same data is feasible, the ILP problem is feasible as well.
  3. If the polyhedron $P$ is unbounded, the ILP problem is unbounded.
  4. If the polyhedron $P$ is bounded, $F$ is convex.
Explanation. For instance $\max\{-x_1 : x_1 \ge 0,\ x_1 \in \Z\}$ has an unbounded $P$ and the optimum $x_1 = 0$: an unbounded polyhedron does not make the objective function unbounded. A bounded polyhedron contains finitely many integer points, not a convex set, and a feasible LP problem can have an infeasible integer version.
pli-f-011 · level teorema · notes
Which of the following statements on ILP problems is correct?
  1. If the LP relaxation is unbounded, the ILP problem is unbounded.
  2. An ILP problem is infeasible if and only if its LP relaxation is infeasible.
  3. If an ILP problem has an optimal solution, it is unique.
  4. An ILP problem has exactly one of the three outcomes: infeasible, unbounded, optimal solution.
Explanation. As in linear programming, there are three outcomes. A feasible relaxation does not guarantee integer points (for example $2x_1 = 1$ has the solution $x_1 = \frac{1}{2}$ and no integer solution), and there can be several optimal solutions.
pli-f-012 · level ipotesi · notes
Consider an ILP problem $\max\{\vet c\T \vet x : \vet x \in F\}$, with feasible region $F = P \cap \Z^n$ and polyhedron $P = \{\vet x \in \R^n : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet x \ge \vet 0\}$. To prove that, if $P$ is bounded and $F \neq \emptyset$, the problem has an optimal solution, what is needed?
  1. The convexity of $F$ and the linearity of the objective function.
  2. The finiteness of $F$ and the existence of the maximum on a finite nonempty set.
  3. An optimal vertex of the LP relaxation that is also integer.
  4. The strong duality of linear programming applied to the relaxation.
Explanation. Finiteness is enough: a bounded polyhedron contains finitely many integer points, and on a finite nonempty set the maximum always exists. $F$ is not convex, and the optimal vertex of the relaxation is in general not integer.
pli-f-013 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that complete enumeration of $n$ binary variables without other constraints examines $2^n$ solutions, what is used?
  1. That every variable takes $2$ values independently of the others (product rule).
  2. That the cube $[0,1]^n$ has exactly $n$ vertices, one per variable.
  3. That the $n$ variables can be ordered in $n!$ different ways.
  4. That the LP relaxation has at most $2^n$ optimal vertices.
Explanation. With two choices for each of the $n$ variables there are $2 \cdot 2 \cdots 2 = 2^n$ combinations: the vertices of the hypercube.
pli-f-131 · level facile · notes
For a maximization ILP problem a heuristic finds the feasible solution $\bar{\vet x}$ of value $14$, and none of the solutions tried does better. What is needed to claim that $\bar{\vet x}$ is optimal?
  1. Nothing: a feasible solution that is never improved is optimal.
  2. An upper (dual) bound on the optimal value $z(\ILP)$ equal to $14$.
  3. That $\bar{\vet x}$ is a vertex of the polyhedron $P$ of the problem.
  4. That the LP relaxation has a fractional optimum of value $14$.
Explanation. A feasible solution gives only the lower bound $z(\ILP) \ge 14$; optimality is certified by an upper bound with the same value.
pli-f-132 · level facile · notes
An ILP problem with $n = 60$ binary variables is to be solved by complete enumeration, evaluating all the $2^n$ solutions. Which statement is correct?
  1. It needs $60^2 = 3600$ evaluations: enumeration is the best choice.
  2. It needs $2 \cdot 60 = 120$ evaluations, one per value of each variable.
  3. It needs $2^{60} \approx 1.15 \cdot 10^{18}$ evaluations: bounds on the optimal value are needed instead.
  4. It needs $60!$ evaluations, as many as the permutations of the variables.
Explanation. $n$ binary variables have $2^n$ combinations; for $n = 60$ they are about $10^{18}$. To avoid enumeration we use primal bounds (feasible solutions) and dual bounds (relaxations).
pli-f-133 · level facile · notes
Consider a maximization ILP problem and the LP problem with the same data without the integrality constraints. Which combination of outcomes is possible?
  1. The LP problem is infeasible and the ILP problem has an optimal solution.
  2. The ILP problem has optimal value $10$ and the LP problem optimal value $8$.
  3. The LP problem is infeasible and the ILP problem is unbounded.
  4. The LP problem has an optimal solution and the ILP problem is infeasible.
Explanation. Every feasible integer solution is feasible for the LP problem, so an infeasible LP problem implies an infeasible ILP problem, and $z(\ILP) \le z(\LP)$. The converse fails: with $2x_1 = 1$ the LP problem has the optimum $x_1 = \frac{1}{2}$ and no integer point is feasible.
pli-f-205 · level facile · notes
In the canonical maximization form of an integer linear programming (ILP) problem, which constraints appear besides $\mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b$?
  1. Only $\vet x \ge \vet 0$; integrality is imposed afterwards, by rounding.
  2. $\vet x \in \{0,1\}^n$, because every integer variable is binary.
  3. $\vet x \ge \vet 0$ and $\vet x$ integer, that is $\vet x \in \Z^n_{\ge 0}$.
  4. $\mat A\,\vet x = \vet b$ and $\vet x \in \Z^n$, with free variables.
Explanation. The canonical form has $\le$ constraints and nonnegative integer variables: $\vet x \in \Z^n_{\ge 0}$.
pli-f-206 · level facile · notes
A linear optimization problem has some integer variables and some continuous ones. What is it called in the terminology of the integer linear programming module?
  1. A MIP problem (mixed integer linear programming).
  2. A BIP problem (binary integer programming).
  3. A pure ILP problem, because at least one variable is integer.
  4. The LP relaxation of the ILP problem.
Explanation. ILP: all variables integer; BIP: all binary; MIP: some integer and some continuous.
pli-f-207 · level teorema · notes
Consider the ILP problem in canonical maximization form $\max\{\vet c\T \vet x : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet x \in \Z^n_{\ge 0}\}$ with $P = \{\vet x \in \R^n : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet x \ge \vet 0\}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The feasible region is the polyhedron $P$, because the constraints are linear.
  2. The feasible region is the convex hull of $P$.
  3. The feasible region is $P \cap \{0,1\}^n$ for every ILP problem.
  4. The feasible region is $F = P \cap \Z^n$, the integer points of the polyhedron $P$.
Explanation. By definition $F = P \cap \Z^n$: the points of the integer lattice that lie in the polyhedron $P$.
pli-f-208 · level facile · notes
In the ILP problem $\max\ x_1 + x_2$ with $2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 5$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$, how many points does the feasible region $F$ have?
  1. $3$.
  2. $6$.
  3. $10$.
  4. Infinitely many, because the polyhedron has positive area.
Explanation. We need $x_1 + x_2 \le 2$: the nonnegative integer points are $(0,0),(1,0),(2,0),(0,1),(1,1),(0,2)$.
pli-f-209 · level facile · notes
In a maximization ILP problem with feasible region $F$, what is the difference between the optimal solution $\tilde{\vet x}(\ILP)$ and the optimal value $z(\ILP)$?
  1. They are the same thing: both denote the maximum of the objective function.
  2. $\tilde{\vet x}(\ILP)$ is a point of $F$ maximizing $\vet c\T \vet x$; $z(\ILP) = \vet c\T \tilde{\vet x}(\ILP)$ is a number.
  3. $z(\ILP)$ is a point of $F$; $\tilde{\vet x}(\ILP)$ is the objective function value.
  4. $\tilde{\vet x}(\ILP)$ is the optimum of the LP relaxation, $z(\ILP)$ that of the ILP problem.
Explanation. The optimal solution is a feasible vector; the optimal value is the objective function value at that vector.
pli-f-210 · level facile · notes
In the ILP problem $\max\ 2x_1 + x_2$ with $x_1 + x_2 \le 2$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$, what are the optimal solution and the optimal value?
  1. $\tilde{\vet x}(\ILP) = (1,1)$ and $z(\ILP) = 3$.
  2. $\tilde{\vet x}(\ILP) = (2,0)$ and $z(\ILP) = 2$.
  3. $\tilde{\vet x}(\ILP) = (0,2)$ and $z(\ILP) = 4$.
  4. $\tilde{\vet x}(\ILP) = (2,0)$ and $z(\ILP) = 4$.
Explanation. Among the points with $x_1 + x_2 \le 2$ the largest value of $2x_1 + x_2$ is $4$, attained only at $(2,0)$.
pli-f-211 · level teorema · notes
For a maximization ILP problem with a nonempty and finite feasible region $F$, which statement about the optimal solution is correct?
  1. An optimal solution always exists, but it need not be unique.
  2. There is always a unique optimal solution, because $F$ is finite.
  3. An optimal solution exists only if the LP relaxation has an integer optimum.
  4. The optimal solution is always a vertex of the constraint polyhedron $P$.
Explanation. On a nonempty finite set the maximum exists; several points can share the maximum value.
pli-f-212 · level ipotesi · notes
We want to prove that a maximization ILP problem has an optimal solution. Which assumption suffices, together with $F \neq \emptyset$?
  1. That the matrix $\mat A$ has all integer coefficients.
  2. That the cost vector $\vet c$ has positive components.
  3. That the constraint polyhedron $P$ is bounded, so $F$ is finite.
  4. That there are at least as many constraints as variables.
Explanation. With $P$ bounded, $F$ is finite and nonempty, so the maximum exists; without boundedness the problem can be unbounded.
pli-f-213 · level facile · notes
In the graphical method for a maximization ILP problem in two variables, how is an optimal solution found?
  1. Find the optimal vertex of the polyhedron and round it to the nearest integer point.
  2. Move the level line against the gradient up to the first integer point.
  3. Pick the integer point of the polyhedron farthest from the origin.
  4. Move the level line of $\vet c\T \vet x$ in the gradient direction up to the last integer point of the polyhedron.
Explanation. The level line moves along $\nabla f = \vet c$; the last lattice point of $P$ it touches is optimal.
pli-f-214 · level facile · notes
In the graphical method for the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$), the last integer point touched by the level line $3x_1 + 2x_2 = z$ is $(4,1)$. What can be concluded?
  1. $(4,1)$ is optimal and $z(\ILP) = 15$, the value of the LP relaxation.
  2. $(4,1)$ is only a primal bound: further checks are needed.
  3. $(4,1)$ is the unique optimal solution and $z(\ILP) = 14$.
  4. The optimal solutions are $(4,1)$ and $(3,2)$, with $z(\ILP) = 13$.
Explanation. No other point of $F$ lies on the line $3x_1 + 2x_2 = 14$, so the optimum is unique.
pli-f-215 · level facile · notes
Complete enumeration of an ILP problem with $n$ binary variables and no other constraint evaluates how many solutions?
  1. $2^n$.
  2. $n^2$.
  3. $n!$.
  4. $2n$.
Explanation. Each variable is $0$ or $1$ independently of the others: $2^n$ combinations.
pli-f-216 · level facile · notes
A student claims that the midpoint of two feasible solutions of an ILP problem is always feasible. Why is this wrong?
  1. The midpoint can violate $\mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b$ even though $P$ is convex.
  2. The feasible region $F = P \cap \Z^n$ is not convex: the midpoint need not be integer.
  3. It is wrong only when the two solutions have the same objective value.
  4. It is not wrong: $F$ is convex because it is an intersection of half-spaces.
Explanation. The midpoint lies in $P$ (convex), but e.g. between $(0,0)$ and $(1,0)$ lies $(\frac12,0) \notin \Z^2$.
pli-f-217 · level facile · notes
An ILP problem has a feasible LP relaxation (same constraints, without integrality). What can be said about the ILP problem?
  1. It is always feasible, because the polyhedron is nonempty.
  2. It is feasible if and only if the polyhedron is bounded.
  3. It can be infeasible: the polyhedron may contain no integer point.
  4. It is feasible and has the same optimal value as the relaxation.
Explanation. For instance $\{x : 2x = 1,\ x \ge 0\}$ is nonempty but contains no integer point.
pli-f-218 · level teorema · notes
What are the possible outcomes of an ILP problem in canonical maximization form (with integer data)?
  1. Infeasible, unbounded, or with an optimal solution.
  2. Only infeasible or with an optimal solution, because $F$ is finite.
  3. With an optimal solution, or with a finite supremum that is not attained.
  4. Infeasible, unbounded, or with infinitely many non-integer optimal solutions.
Explanation. They are the same three outcomes as in linear programming; $F$ is finite only when the polyhedron is bounded.
pli-f-219 · level ipotesi · notes
We want to prove that complete enumeration terminates on an ILP problem. Which assumption is needed?
  1. That the problem has at most two variables, so it can be drawn.
  2. That the LP relaxation has an integer optimal solution.
  3. That all coefficients of the objective function are positive.
  4. That the feasible region $F$ is finite, e.g. because the polyhedron is bounded.
Explanation. Enumeration evaluates every point of $F$: it terminates only if $F$ is a finite set.
pli-f-220 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that a feasible solution $\bar{\vet x}$ of a maximization ILP problem is optimal, without enumerating, what is needed?
  1. That $\bar{\vet x}$ was found by the best available heuristic.
  2. A dual bound $\UB$ with $\UB = \vet c\T \bar{\vet x}$.
  3. That $\bar{\vet x}$ is close to the optimal solution of the LP relaxation.
  4. A second feasible point with value lower than $\vet c\T \bar{\vet x}$.
Explanation. From $\vet c\T \bar{\vet x} \le z(\ILP) \le \UB$ with $\UB = \vet c\T \bar{\vet x}$ we get $z(\ILP) = \vet c\T \bar{\vet x}$.
pli-f-221 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the outcomes of an ILP problem and of its LP relaxation (same constraints, real $\vet x \ge \vet 0$) is correct?
  1. If the LP relaxation is feasible, the ILP problem is feasible too.
  2. If the ILP problem is feasible, the LP relaxation is feasible too.
  3. If the LP relaxation is unbounded, the ILP problem is unbounded.
  4. The two problems always have the same outcome.
Explanation. $F \subseteq P$: a feasible integer point is also a point of the polyhedron. The converse fails.

2. Primal and dual bounds (52)

pli-f-014 · level facile · notes
In a maximization ILP problem with optimal value $z(\ILP)$, what are primal bounds and dual bounds?
  1. Primal bounds (values of feasible solutions) are lower bounds; dual bounds are upper bounds.
  2. Primal bounds are upper bounds; dual bounds are lower bounds.
  3. Both are upper bounds on $z(\ILP)$.
  4. Primal bounds come from the LP relaxation, dual bounds from heuristics.
Explanation. The value of a feasible solution does not exceed the optimum (lower bound); a dual bound holds for all feasible solutions (upper bound).
pli-f-015 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem $\max\{\vet c\T \vet x : \vet x \in F\}$ and $R \subseteq \R^n$. The problem $\max\{\vet c\T \vet x : \vet x \in R\}$ is a relaxation of the ILP problem if:
  1. $R \subseteq F$
  2. $F \subseteq R$
  3. $R \cap F \neq \emptyset$
  4. $R$ is convex
Explanation. A relaxation has a larger region with the same objective function: this is why $z(\ILP) \le z(R)$.
pli-f-016 · level facile · notes
Let $z(\ILP)$ be the optimal value of a maximization ILP problem. Is the value of a feasible (non-optimal) solution of its LP relaxation a bound on $z(\ILP)$?
  1. Yes, an upper bound.
  2. Yes, a lower bound.
  3. In general it is neither a lower bound nor an upper bound.
  4. Yes, an upper bound if the solution is a vertex.
Explanation. Only the optimal value of the relaxation (or the value of a dual feasible solution) is an upper bound; a fractional LP solution is not feasible for the ILP problem.
pli-f-017 · level facile · notes
A maximization MIP problem, with optimal value $z(\MIP)$, has integer costs and one continuous variable; its LP relaxation has optimal value $z(\LP) = \frac{47}{2}$. What is the best upper bound on $z(\MIP)$ that can be derived?
  1. $23$
  2. $24$
  3. $22$
  4. $\frac{47}{2}$
Explanation. With a continuous variable the value of a feasible solution is not necessarily integer: the rounding $\lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor$ is not valid and the bound stays $z(\LP)$.
pli-f-018 · level facile · notes
In a maximization ILP problem, when is a feasible solution $\bar{\vet x}$ certified optimal?
  1. When the gap between its value and a dual bound (upper bound) is zero.
  2. When it is obtained by rounding the optimum of the LP relaxation.
  3. When a local search heuristic finds no better solution.
  4. When it satisfies all the constraints of the problem with equality.
Explanation. A bound from the other side is needed: if the value of $\bar{\vet x}$ (lower bound) equals an upper bound, no solution can be better.
pli-f-019 · level facile · notes
What is obtained by rounding the optimal solution of the LP relaxation of an ILP problem?
  1. An optimal solution, if every variable is rounded to the nearest integer.
  2. An integer point that may be infeasible and, if feasible, not optimal.
  3. Always a feasible solution, which however is in general not optimal.
  4. An upper bound on the optimal value of the maximization ILP problem.
Explanation. In the example $\max\ x_1 + 0.64 x_2$, $50x_1 + 31x_2 \le 250$, $3x_1 - 2x_2 \ge -4$ the optimum of the relaxation is $(\frac{376}{193}, \frac{950}{193})$: rounding to the nearest point $(2,5)$ is infeasible, and the integer optimum $(5,0)$ is far away.
pli-f-020 · level facile · notes
For the ILP problem $\max\{\sum_j c_j x_j : \sum_j a_{ij} x_j \le b_i\ (i = 1, \dots, m),\ \vet x \in \Z^n_{\ge 0}\}$ the constraints multiplied by $\pi_i$ are summed to obtain the upper bound $\sum_i b_i \pi_i$. Which conditions must the multipliers satisfy?
  1. $\pi_i$ of any sign and $\sum_i a_{ij}\pi_i = c_j$ for every $j$.
  2. $\pi_i \ge 0$ and $\sum_i a_{ij}\pi_i \le c_j$ for every $j$.
  3. $\pi_i \ge 0$ and $\sum_i a_{ij}\pi_i \ge c_j$ for every $j$.
  4. $\pi_i \in \Z_{\ge 0}$ for every $i$, with no other condition.
Explanation. A negative multiplier would reverse the inequality; the combined coefficients must be at least the costs so that $\sum_j c_j x_j \le \sum_i b_i \pi_i$ with $\vet x \ge \vet 0$.
pli-f-021 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the LP relaxation has the optimum $(\frac{7}{2}, \frac{5}{2})$ of value $\frac{31}{2}$, and $(4,1)$ is feasible. Which bounds do we have on the optimal value $z(\ILP)$?
  1. $14 \le z(\ILP) \le \frac{31}{2}$
  2. $13 \le z(\ILP) \le 16$
  3. $z(\ILP) = 15$
  4. $14 \le z(\ILP) \le 15$
Explanation. $(4,1)$ gives the lower bound $14$; costs and variables are integer, so the upper bound $\frac{31}{2}$ is rounded down to $15$. The gap is $\frac{1}{14}$: $(4,1)$ is not yet certified optimal.
pli-f-022 · level media · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the optimal solution of the dual of the LP relaxation (multipliers of the two constraints) is $(\frac{17}{62}, \frac{2}{31})$. What is the best integer upper bound on the optimal value $z(\ILP)$ obtained from it?
  1. $15$
  2. $\frac{31}{2}$
  3. $16$
  4. $14$
Explanation. $45 \cdot \frac{17}{62} + 49 \cdot \frac{2}{31} = \frac{31}{2} = z(\LP)$ (strong duality); with integer costs and variables it is rounded down to $15$. The value $14$ is not certified by these multipliers.
pli-f-023 · level teorema · notes
Let $z(\ILP)$ be the optimal value of a maximization ILP problem and $z(\LP)$ the optimal value of its LP relaxation (same constraints, without integrality). Let $\tilde{\vet x}(\LP)$ be an optimal solution of the relaxation. Which statement is correct?
  1. $z(\ILP) \ge z(\LP)$, because the ILP problem has more constraints and hence a higher value.
  2. $z(\ILP) \le z(\LP)$, and if an optimal solution of the relaxation is integer, it is optimal for the ILP problem.
  3. If $z(\LP)$ is integer, then $\tilde{\vet x}(\LP)$ is integer and optimal for the ILP problem.
  4. $z(\ILP) = \lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor$ for every ILP problem with integer data, because the costs are integer.
Explanation. Every integer solution is feasible for the relaxation, which has the same objective function. In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$) $\lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor = 15 > 14 = z(\ILP)$.
pli-f-024 · level teorema · notes
Let $z(\LP)$ be the optimal value of the LP relaxation of a maximization problem $\max\{\vet c\T \vet x : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet x \ge \vet 0\}$ with integer variables (optimal value $z(\ILP)$) or partly continuous ones (optimal value $z(\MIP)$). Which statement is correct?
  1. If all the variables are integer, $z(\ILP) \le \lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor$ whatever the costs.
  2. If $\mat A$ and $\vet b$ are integer, $z(\MIP) \le \lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor$ also with continuous variables.
  3. If all the variables and all the costs are integer, $z(\ILP) \le \lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor$.
  4. It always holds that $z(\ILP) \le \lceil z(\LP) \rceil - 1$.
Explanation. With integer variables and costs the value of every feasible solution is integer and does not exceed $z(\LP)$, hence it does not exceed $\lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor$; in the other cases rounding is not valid.
pli-f-025 · level ipotesi · notes
In an ILP problem with constraints $\sum_k a_{ik} x_k \le b_i$ and $\vet x \ge \vet 0$ we want to prove that every feasible solution satisfies $x_j \le \lfloor b_i / a_{ij} \rfloor$. Which hypotheses are needed?
  1. Only $a_{ij} > 0$, with no other hypothesis on constraint $i$.
  2. $b_i \ge 0$ and a bounded LP relaxation.
  3. Integer costs $c_j$ and $a_{ij} > 0$.
  4. $a_{ik} \ge 0$ for every $k$, $a_{ij} > 0$, $\vet x \ge \vet 0$ and $x_j$ integer.
Explanation. With non-negative coefficients and variables $a_{ij} x_j \le \sum_k a_{ik} x_k \le b_i$; the integrality of $x_j$ allows rounding down. With a negative coefficient in the constraint the step fails.
pli-f-026 · level ipotesi · notes
For the ILP problem $\max\{\sum_j c_j x_j : \sum_j a_{ij} x_j \le b_i\ (i = 1, \dots, m),\ \vet x \in \Z^n_{\ge 0}\}$, with optimal value $z(\ILP)$, we want to prove $z(\ILP) \le \sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i$ for a vector of multipliers $\bar{\vet\pi}$. What is needed?
  1. $\bar{\vet \pi} \ge \vet 0$, $\sum_i a_{ij} \bar\pi_i \ge c_j$ for every $j$, and $\vet x \ge \vet 0$.
  2. $\bar{\vet \pi}$ of any sign with $\sum_i a_{ij} \bar\pi_i = c_j$ for every $j$.
  3. $\bar{\vet \pi} \ge \vet 0$ and $\sum_i a_{ij} \bar\pi_i \le c_j$ for every $j$.
  4. $\bar{\vet \pi} \ge \vet 0$ and the integrality of the variables $x_j$.
Explanation. The constraints are multiplied by $\bar\pi_i \ge 0$ and summed; with $\vet x \ge \vet 0$ and combined coefficients at least equal to the costs, the left-hand side bounds $\sum_j c_j x_j$ from above. Integrality is not needed.
pli-f-134 · level facile · notes
For the ILP problem $\max\ 5x_1 + 3x_2$, $6x_1 + 4x_2 \le 25$, $2x_1 + 5x_2 \le 18$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$, the one-variable heuristic sets a single variable to its maximum value (the other one to $0$) and keeps the better of the two solutions. Which primal bound $\LB$ does it give?
  1. $\LB = 20$, with the solution $(4, 0)$.
  2. $\LB = 9$, with the solution $(0, 3)$.
  3. $\LB = 45$, with the solution $(9, 0)$.
  4. $\LB = \frac{125}{6}$, with the solution $(\frac{25}{6}, 0)$.
Explanation. Only $x_1$: $\min\{\lfloor \frac{25}{6} \rfloor, \lfloor \frac{18}{2} \rfloor\} = 4$, value $20$. Only $x_2$: $\min\{\lfloor \frac{25}{4} \rfloor, \lfloor \frac{18}{5} \rfloor\} = 3$, value $9$. We keep $20$; $(9,0)$ violates the first constraint and $\frac{25}{6}$ is not integer.

For a maximization ILP problem with optimal value $z(\ILP)$, a heuristic algorithm returns a feasible solution of value $37$. What can be concluded?
  1. That $z(\ILP) \le 37$: the value is a dual bound.
  2. That $z(\ILP) = 37$, if the heuristic was run to the end.
  3. That $z(\ILP) \ge 37$: the value is a primal bound.
  4. Nothing about $z(\ILP)$, until the LP relaxation is known.
Explanation. Every feasible solution has value at most $z(\ILP)$; a heuristic gives no guarantee of optimality, so its value is only a lower bound.
pli-f-136 · level facile · notes

What does the example show?
  1. Rounding the LP optimum down always gives the integer optimum.
  2. The nearest integer point, $(2, 5)$, is feasible and therefore optimal.
  3. The bound $z(\LP)$ is useless, because the LP optimum is far from $(5, 0)$.
  4. The integer optimum can be far from the LP optimum, even if $z(\LP)$ remains a good bound.
Explanation. No rounding of $(1.94, 4.92)$ gives $(5,0)$; $(2,5)$ violates the first constraint ($255 > 250$). The value, instead, is close: $125 \le 127$.
pli-f-137 · level facile · notes
With the constraints of the car example ($10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) and the objective $\max\ x_1 - x_2$, the LP relaxation has the optimum $(\frac{9}{2}, 0)$. The upper bound $x_1 \le \lfloor \frac{45}{10} \rfloor = 4$ is added. What happens?
  1. Nothing: upper bounds on the variables never cut off the LP optimum.
  2. The new LP optimum is $(4, 0)$, integer and hence optimal; the rounded bound was already $4$.
  3. The LP value goes from $\frac{9}{2}$ to $4$ and the rounded bound from $5$ to $4$.
  4. The bound cuts off the feasible integer point $(4, 1)$ and changes the integer optimum.
Explanation. $(\frac{9}{2}, 0)$ violates $x_1 \le 4$, and the new optimum $(4,0)$ has value $4$. The rounded bound was $\lfloor \frac{9}{2} \rfloor = 4$ already: a better formulation does not always improve the rounded bound.

  2. Yes, but only for the LP relaxation, not for the integer points.
  3. No: the bound holds only if $\frac{7}{3}$ is an integer.

  3. $52 \le z(\ILP) \le 53$
  4. $53 \le z(\ILP) \le 55$

  2. With well chosen multipliers one goes below $z(\LP)$, down to $z(\ILP)$.
  3. The optimal value of the dual problem is equal to $z(\ILP)$.
  4. The sign of the multipliers does not matter if the combined coefficients suffice.
Explanation. The conditions on the multipliers are the constraints of the dual of the LP relaxation; by strong duality the minimum of $\vet b\T\vet\pi$ is $z(\LP) \ge z(\ILP)$. For $\le$ constraints the multipliers must be non-negative.

  2. $10\%$.
  3. $80\%$.
  4. $25\%$.
Explanation. $(50 - 40)/40 = 0.25$. The wrong $20\%$ divides by $\UB$ instead of $|\LB|$.

In a maximization ILP problem with optimal value $z(\ILP)$, what is the difference between a primal bound $\LB$ and a dual bound $\UB$?
  1. $\LB$ is the value of one feasible solution; $\UB$ bounds the value of all feasible solutions from above.
  2. $\LB$ bounds all feasible solutions from above; $\UB$ is the value of one feasible solution.
  3. Both are values of feasible solutions, one of the ILP problem and one of the relaxation.
  4. $\LB$ comes from the dual problem, $\UB$ from the primal problem.
Explanation. In a maximization problem primal bounds are lower bounds and dual bounds are upper bounds: $\LB \le z(\ILP) \le \UB$.

To get a dual bound on a canonical maximization ILP problem, the $\le$ constraints are combined with multipliers $\pi_i$. Which mistake makes the bound invalid?

Explanation. The multipliers of $\le$ constraints must be nonnegative and the combined coefficients at least equal to the costs.
pli-f-247 · level facile · notes
For the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) we have the feasible solution $(3,2)$ of value $13$ and the LP relaxation with $z(\LP) = \frac{31}{2}$. Which interval contains $z(\ILP)$, using the legitimate rounding?
  1. $13 \le z(\ILP) \le 15$.
  2. $13 \le z(\ILP) \le 15.5$.
  3. $13 \le z(\ILP) \le 16$.
  4. $14 \le z(\ILP) \le 15$.
Explanation. Integer variables and costs: $z(\ILP) \le \lfloor 15.5 \rfloor = 15$; the lower bound is the value $13$ of the known solution.
pli-f-248 · level teorema · notes
For a maximization ILP problem, which statement about the dual $\mathrm{D(LP)}$ of the LP relaxation is correct?
  1. The optimal value of the dual always equals $z(\ILP)$.
  2. The dual gives bounds better than $z(\LP)$ when the variables are integer.
  3. The best dual bound obtainable with constraint multipliers is $z(\mathrm{D(LP)}) = z(\LP)$.
  4. The dual gives a lower bound on $z(\ILP)$.
Explanation. By strong duality of the relaxation the best multipliers give exactly $z(\LP)$, not less.
pli-f-249 · level facile · notes
The problem $\max\{g(\vet x) : \vet x \in R\}$ is a relaxation of $\max\{f(\vet x) : \vet x \in F\}$. Which conditions define it?
  1. $R \subseteq F$ and $g(\vet x) \le f(\vet x)$ for every $\vet x \in R$.
  2. $F \subseteq R$ and $g = f$ on all of $R$.
  3. $R = F$ and $g(\vet x) \ge f(\vet x)$ for every $\vet x$.
  4. $F \subseteq R$ and $g(\vet x) \ge f(\vet x)$ for every $\vet x \in F$.
Explanation. A relaxation has more solutions and an objective that does not underestimate: its optimum is an upper bound.
pli-f-556 · level facile · notes
What is the LP relaxation of the ILP problem $\max\ 2x_1 + 3x_2$, $x_1 + x_2 \le 4$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$?
  1. $\max\ 2x_1 + 3x_2$, $x_1 + x_2 \le 4$, $x_1, x_2 \in \{0,1\}$.
  2. $\max\ 2x_1 + 3x_2$, $x_1 + x_2 \le 4$, real $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$.
  3. $\max\ 2x_1 + 3x_2$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$, with no constraints.
  4. $\min\ 4\pi$, $\pi \ge 2$, $\pi \ge 3$.
Explanation. Only the integrality constraints are removed. The last option is the dual, not the relaxation.
pli-f-557 · level ipotesi · notes
If the LP relaxation of an ILP problem has an integer optimal solution, it is optimal for the ILP problem too. What is needed to prove it?
  1. That the relaxation has a unique optimal solution.
  2. That it is feasible for the ILP problem.
  3. That the costs are positive.
  4. That the dual has an integer solution.
Explanation. Its value is $z(\LP)$, an upper bound on $z(\ILP)$, attained by a point of $F$.
pli-f-558 · level facile · notes
The LP relaxation of a maximization ILP problem with non-integer costs has $z(\LP) = 12.5$, and a feasible integer solution is worth $11.8$. What is known about $z(\ILP)$?
  1. $11.8 \le z(\ILP) \le 12$.
  2. $z(\ILP) = 12.5$.
  3. $z(\ILP) \ge 12.5$.
  4. $11.8 \le z(\ILP) \le 12.5$.
Explanation. With non-integer costs $z(\LP)$ is not rounded; the lower bound is the value of the known solution.

Part: Strengthening the formulation (129)

3. Ideal formulations (36)

pli-f-027 · level facile · notes
What is the convex hull $\mathrm{conv}(S)$ of a set $S \subseteq \R^n$?
  1. The set of the linear combinations of points of $S$.
  2. The set of all the convex combinations of points of $S$.
  3. The smallest bounded polyhedron containing $S$ and its integer points.
  4. The set of the integer points of $S$.
Explanation. A convex combination has weights $\lambda_h \ge 0$ with $\sum_h \lambda_h = 1$; $\mathrm{conv}(S)$ is the smallest convex set containing $S$.
pli-f-028 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the finite set of feasible solutions of an ILP problem. What is known about the vertices of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$?
  1. They can be fractional points.
  2. They coincide with all the points of $F$.
  3. They are points of $F$, hence feasible integer solutions.
  4. They are the vertices of the polyhedron of the LP relaxation.
Explanation. The vertices of the hull of a finite set are points of the set; the other points of $F$ lie on the boundary or in the interior.
pli-f-029 · level facile · notes
When is a polyhedron $P = \{\vet x \in \R^n : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet x \ge \vet 0\}$ a formulation of an ILP problem with feasible region $F$?
  1. When $P \cap \Z^n = F$.
  2. When $P = F$.
  3. When $P \subseteq \mathrm{conv}(F)$.
  4. When all the vertices of $P$ are integer.
Explanation. The formulations of the same problem have the same integer points and differ in the fractional points.
pli-f-030 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem and $P$ a formulation of it (a polyhedron with $P \cap \Z^n = F$). Which relation holds between $P$ and the convex hull $\mathrm{conv}(F)$?
  1. $P \subseteq \mathrm{conv}(F)$
  2. $P \cap \mathrm{conv}(F) = \emptyset$
  3. $P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$ always
  4. $\mathrm{conv}(F) \subseteq P$
Explanation. $P$ is convex and contains $F$, so it contains the smallest convex set containing $F$.
pli-f-031 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem. When is a formulation $P$ (a polyhedron with $P \cap \Z^n = F$) ideal?
  1. When the optimum of the relaxation is integer for the given objective function.
  2. When $P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$.
  3. When $P$ has the smallest possible number of constraints.
  4. When $P$ is bounded and has at least one integer vertex.
Explanation. Ideal means that the region of the relaxation is exactly the convex hull of the integer solutions; an integer optimum for one objective is not enough.
pli-f-032 · level facile · notes
Two formulations $P_1 \subseteq P_2$ of the same maximization ILP problem have LP relaxations with optimal values $z(\LP_1)$ and $z(\LP_2)$; $z(\ILP)$ is the optimal value of the problem. What holds?
  1. $z(\LP_1) \ge z(\LP_2)$
  2. $z(\LP_1) = z(\LP_2)$
  3. $z(\ILP) \le z(\LP_1) \le z(\LP_2)$
  4. $z(\LP_1) = z(\ILP)$
Explanation. $P_1$ is stronger: the maximum over a smaller set is not larger, and every formulation contains the integer solutions.
pli-f-033 · level facile · notes
How many vertices does the convex hull of the $16$ feasible integer points of the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) have?
  1. $16$
  2. $4$
  3. $5$
  4. $6$
Explanation. The vertices are $(0,0)$, $(4,0)$, $(4,1)$, $(3,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(0,3)$; of the other $10$ points five lie on the boundary and five in the interior.
pli-f-034 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$), let $P$ be the polyhedron of the two constraints with $\vet x \ge \vet 0$ and $F$ the set of feasible integer points. Why is $P$ not an ideal formulation, that is $P \neq \mathrm{conv}(F)$?
  1. Because the point $(\frac{7}{2}, \frac{5}{2})$ of $P$ violates $x_1 + x_2 \le 5$, valid for $\mathrm{conv}(F)$.
  2. Because the ILP problem has several optimal solutions and the relaxation only one.
  3. Because some points of $F$ lie on the boundary of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ without being vertices.
  4. Because $P$ is bounded and hence contains only finitely many integer points.
Explanation. One point of $P$ outside the hull is enough: the optimum of the relaxation $(\frac{7}{2}, \frac{5}{2})$ has sum $6 > 5$.
pli-f-035 · level media · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$), the convex hull of the feasible integer points has vertices $(0,0)$, $(4,0)$, $(4,1)$, $(3,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(0,3)$. How many of the $16$ feasible integer points lie in the interior (not on the boundary) of the hull?
  1. $10$
  2. $5$
  3. $6$
  4. $0$
Explanation. On the boundary, besides the vertices, lie $(0,1)$, $(0,2)$, $(1,0)$, $(2,0)$, $(3,0)$; in the interior $(1,1)$, $(2,1)$, $(3,1)$, $(1,2)$, $(2,2)$. A point that is not a vertex can lie on the boundary.
pli-f-036 · level teorema · notes
Let $P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$ be an ideal, bounded formulation of a maximization ILP problem with feasible region $F \neq \emptyset$ and optimal value $z(\ILP)$; $z(\LP)$ is the optimal value of the relaxation on $P$. Which statement is correct?
  1. Every optimal solution of the relaxation is integer, even with several optima.
  2. $P$ is ideal if and only if the relaxation has an integer optimum for one objective.
  3. The relaxation has an optimal vertex, every optimal vertex is optimal for the ILP problem, and $z(\LP) = z(\ILP)$.
  4. $z(\LP) = z(\ILP)$ for the given objective is enough to say that $P$ is ideal.
Explanation. The vertices of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ are integer, and a nonempty bounded polyhedron has an optimal vertex. With several optimal solutions a fractional point of the optimal face can be optimal: this is why the statement speaks of vertices.
pli-f-037 · level teorema · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem. An ideal formulation is a polyhedron $P$ with $P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$. Which statement about its existence is correct?
  1. It exists only if $F$ is bounded, and it uses at most $2n$ inequalities.
  2. It does not exist when $F$ is infinite, even with rational data.
  3. It is always obtained by adding upper bounds on the variables.
  4. With rational data it always exists, but it can require very many inequalities.
Explanation. By Meyer's theorem, with rational data the convex hull of the integer points is a polyhedron, described by finitely many inequalities, which can however be exponentially many; finding them is in general as hard as solving the problem.
pli-f-038 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$ be an ideal, nonempty and bounded formulation of a maximization ILP problem ($z(\ILP)$ optimal value, $z(\LP)$ optimal value of the relaxation on $P$). To prove that an optimal vertex of the relaxation is optimal for the ILP problem, what is needed?
  1. An optimal vertex exists, the vertices of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ lie in $F$, and $z(\ILP) \le z(\LP)$.
  2. Strong duality and the complementary slackness conditions on the relaxation.
  3. The convexity of $F$ and the linearity of the objective function.
  4. Bland's rule, which leads the simplex to an integer optimal vertex.
Explanation. The optimal vertex is a feasible integer solution of value $z(\LP)$, and no integer solution exceeds $z(\LP)$.
pli-f-039 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $P_1 \subseteq P_2$ be two formulations of the same maximization ILP problem and $z(\LP_1)$, $z(\LP_2)$ the optimal values of the LP relaxations on them. To prove that $z(\LP_1) \le z(\LP_2)$, what is needed?
  1. That $P_1$ and $P_2$ are both ideal formulations.
  2. The same objective function: the maximum on a subset is not larger.
  3. That both polyhedra are bounded and have integer vertices.
  4. That the region of the dual of $\LP_1$ is contained in that of the dual of $\LP_2$.
Explanation. It is a comparison of maxima of the same function over nested sets; no integrality assumption is needed.
pli-f-142 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem. Finding the ideal formulation $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is in general very difficult. What is done in practice?
  1. The formulation is replaced by the polyhedron of the non-negativity constraints only.
  2. All the points of $F$ are enumerated and their convex hull is computed.
  3. The given formulation is kept and valid inequalities that cut off fractional points are added.
  4. The solution of the LP relaxation is rounded and declared optimal.
Explanation. The hull can need exponentially many inequalities; the formulation is strengthened step by step with inequalities satisfied by all the points of $F$.
pli-f-143 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the convex hull of the feasible integer points has vertices $(0,0)$, $(4,0)$, $(4,1)$, $(3,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(0,3)$. Where are the feasible points $(0,1)$ and $(3,0)$, which are not vertices?
  1. Strictly inside the hull.
  2. On the boundary of the hull, on two edges.
  3. Outside the hull, because they are not vertices.
  4. They are vertices as well, only hidden by the edges.
Explanation. $(0,1)$ lies on the edge from $(0,0)$ to $(0,3)$ and $(3,0)$ on the edge from $(0,0)$ to $(4,0)$: a point that is not a vertex can lie on the boundary.
pli-f-144 · level facile · notes
Let $P$ be a formulation of an ILP problem with feasible region $F$ (a polyhedron with $P \cap \Z^n = F$). For the objective $\vet c$ the LP relaxation on $P$ has an integer optimum. Can we conclude that $P$ is ideal ($P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$)?
  1. Yes: an integer optimum proves that all the vertices of $P$ are integer.
  2. Yes, if the integer optimum is unique.
  3. No: an ideal formulation never has integer optima.
  4. No: for another objective the optimum of the relaxation on $P$ can be fractional.
Explanation. A formulation is ideal if all its vertices are integer, that is if the relaxation has an integer optimum for every objective; one objective is not enough.
pli-f-145 · level facile · notes
Let $F \neq \emptyset$ be the finite feasible region of an ILP problem and $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ its convex hull. Which statement is <u>false</u>?
  1. The vertices of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ are feasible integer solutions.
  2. $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ contains fractional points that are not convex combinations of points of $F$.
  3. $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is contained in every formulation of the problem.
  4. $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is a polyhedron described by finitely many inequalities.
Explanation. By definition $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is the set of the convex combinations of the points of $F$; it is the smallest convex set containing $F$, so it lies in every formulation, and it is a polyhedron with vertices in $F$.
pli-f-146 · level facile · notes
Let $P$ be a formulation of an ILP problem with feasible region $F$ ($P \cap \Z^n = F$). Inequalities are added to $P$ to strengthen it. How far can the polyhedron shrink while remaining a formulation?
  1. Down to the set $F$ itself, which is a polyhedron.
  2. Down to the optimal point only, removing the other points of $F$.
  3. Down to $\mathrm{conv}(F)$, never removing points of $F$.
  4. It cannot shrink: every additional inequality removes points of $F$.
Explanation. A formulation must contain all the points of $F$, hence it contains $\mathrm{conv}(F)$, the strongest formulation. $F$ is a set of isolated points, not a polyhedron (if it has more than one point).
pli-f-250 · level facile · notes
What is the convex hull $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ of the finite set $F$ of integer solutions of an ILP problem?
  1. The set of all linear combinations of the points of $F$.
  2. The constraint polyhedron $\mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet x \ge \vet 0$.
  3. The set of all convex combinations of the points of $F$.
  4. The set of integer points of the constraint polyhedron.
Explanation. $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is the smallest convex set containing $F$: the combinations with nonnegative weights summing to $1$.
pli-f-251 · level teorema · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem; a formulation is a polyhedron $P$ with $P \cap \Z^n = F$, and $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is the convex hull of $F$. Which statement about the vertices of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is correct?
  1. Every vertex of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is a point of $F$, hence integer.
  2. Every point of $F$ is a vertex of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$.
  3. The vertices of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ coincide with those of $P$.
  4. $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ can have fractional vertices if $P$ has them.
Explanation. A vertex is not a convex combination of other points of the set, so it must be one of the generating points; the converse fails (points of $F$ on the boundary or inside).
pli-f-252 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem; a formulation is a polyhedron $P$ with $P \cap \Z^n = F$, and $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is the convex hull of $F$. When is $P$ called ideal?
  1. When $P \cap \Z^n = F$.
  2. When $P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$.
  3. When $P$ has at least one integer vertex.
  4. When the LP relaxation over $P$ has an integer optimum for the given objective.
Explanation. Every formulation satisfies $P \cap \Z^n = F$; the ideal one coincides with the convex hull.
pli-f-253 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem; a formulation is a polyhedron $P$ with $P \cap \Z^n = F$, and $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is the convex hull of $F$. To conclude that $P$ is ideal, which check is sufficient?
  1. That the optimum of the LP relaxation over $P$ is integer for the objective of the problem.
  2. That $P$ is bounded and contains $F$.
  3. That all vertices of $P$ are integer.
  4. That $P$ has as many constraints as $\mathrm{conv}(F)$.
Explanation. If every vertex of $P$ lies in $F$, then $P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$; an integer optimum for a single objective is not enough.
pli-f-254 · level teorema · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem; a formulation is a polyhedron $P$ with $P \cap \Z^n = F$, and $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is the convex hull of $F$. What does the theorem on the LP relaxation over an ideal formulation state (with $F$ nonempty and $P$ bounded)?
  1. The LP relaxation over $P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$ has an integer optimal solution and $z(\LP) = z(\ILP)$.
  2. Every optimal solution of the LP relaxation over $P$ is integer, for every objective.
  3. The LP relaxation over $P$ gives $z(\LP) = \lfloor z(\ILP) \rfloor$.
  4. The LP relaxation over $P$ is infeasible if $F$ has more than one point.
Explanation. The optimum of a bounded LP is attained at a vertex, and the vertices of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ are integer; with multiple optima there are fractional optima too.
pli-f-255 · level ipotesi · notes
In the proof that the LP relaxation over an ideal formulation $P = \mathrm{conv}(F)$ has an integer optimum, which property of linear programming is used?
  1. Every LP has a unique optimal solution.
  2. The dual of an LP always has an integer solution.
  3. Every point of a polyhedron is a vertex.
  4. A bounded LP has an optimum at a vertex.
Explanation. The optimum is attained at a vertex of $\mathrm{conv}(F)$, and those vertices are points of $F$.
pli-f-256 · level facile · notes
$P_1$ and $P_2$ are formulations of the same maximization ILP problem with $P_1 \subseteq P_2$; $z(\LP_k)$ is the optimal value of the LP relaxation over $P_k$. Which relation holds?
  1. $z(\ILP) \le z(\LP_2) \le z(\LP_1)$.
  2. $z(\ILP) \le z(\LP_1) \le z(\LP_2)$.
  3. $z(\LP_1) \le z(\ILP) \le z(\LP_2)$.
  4. $z(\LP_1) = z(\LP_2)$, because they have the same integer points.
Explanation. The maximum over a smaller set is not larger: the stronger formulation gives an upper bound that is not worse.
pli-f-257 · level facile · notes
Given two formulations $P_1$ and $P_2$ of the same ILP problem, when is $P_1$ stronger than $P_2$?
  1. When $P_1$ has more constraints than $P_2$.
  2. When $P_1 \subseteq P_2$.
  3. When $P_1 \supseteq P_2$.
  4. When $P_1$ contains more integer points than $P_2$.
Explanation. Stronger means a smaller polyhedron; the integer points are the same in all formulations.
pli-f-258 · level teorema · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem; a formulation is a polyhedron $P$ with $P \cap \Z^n = F$, and $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is the convex hull of $F$. Which statement about the ideal formulation is correct?

  2. It cannot be compared with the other formulations, because it has different constraints.
  3. It is the formulation with the fewest constraints.
  4. It is stronger than every other formulation, because $\mathrm{conv}(F) \subseteq P$ for every formulation $P$.

pli-f-259 · level facile · notes
For the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the original formulation gives the upper bound $\lfloor \frac{31}{2} \rfloor = 15$. Which upper bound does the ideal formulation give?

  3. $13$, the value of rounding down.
  4. $\frac{31}{2}$, without rounding.

pli-f-260 · level facile · notes
Does an ideal formulation always exist for an ILP problem with integer (or rational) data?
  1. No: it exists only if the problem has two variables.
  2. Yes, and it is always the given formulation, if the data are integer.
  3. Yes: the convex hull of the integer points is a polyhedron (Meyer's theorem).
  4. No: the convex hull always needs infinitely many constraints.
Explanation. $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is described by finitely many linear inequalities, but a huge number may be needed.
pli-f-261 · level facile · notes
Why, in practice, does one not write the ideal formulation of an ILP problem and solve a single LP?
  1. Because the ideal formulation does not exist for bounded problems.
  2. Because the LP relaxation of the ideal formulation is infeasible.
  3. Because the ideal formulation gives a worse upper bound.
  4. It may need very many inequalities, hard to find.
Explanation. One starts from the available formulation and adds valid inequalities that cut off fractional points.
pli-f-262 · level facile · notes
The LP relaxation of a formulation $P$ of an ILP problem has an integer optimum for the given objective. Can we conclude that $P$ is ideal?
  1. Yes: an integer optimum implies that all vertices are integer.
  2. Yes, if the problem has a unique optimal solution.
  3. No: for another objective the optimum could be fractional.
  4. No: an ideal formulation never has integer optima.
Explanation. Being ideal concerns all vertices of $P$, not only the one optimal for one objective.
pli-f-263 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem; a formulation is a polyhedron $P$ with $P \cap \Z^n = F$, and $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is the convex hull of $F$. An inequality that removes a point of $F$ is added to $P$. What is obtained?
  1. No longer a formulation of the same problem: a feasible integer point has been lost.
  2. A stronger formulation of the same problem.
  3. The ideal formulation, if the removed point is not optimal.
  4. An equivalent formulation, because the objective does not change.
Explanation. A formulation must keep all of $F$: $P$ may shrink only down to $\mathrm{conv}(F)$.
pli-f-264 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem; a formulation is a polyhedron $P$ with $P \cap \Z^n = F$, and $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ is the convex hull of $F$. Which chain of inclusions holds for every formulation $P$?
  1. $\mathrm{conv}(F) \subseteq F \subseteq P$.
  2. $F \subseteq \mathrm{conv}(F) \subseteq P$.
  3. $F \subseteq P \subseteq \mathrm{conv}(F)$.
  4. $P \subseteq \mathrm{conv}(F) \subseteq F$.
Explanation. $P$ is convex and contains $F$, so it contains the smallest convex set containing $F$.
pli-f-265 · level ipotesi · notes
Two formulations $P_1 \subseteq P_2$ of the same maximization ILP problem. To prove $z(\LP_1) \le z(\LP_2)$, what is needed?
  1. That $P_1$ is the ideal formulation.
  2. That both relaxations have integer optima.

  4. That $P_2$ has more vertices than $P_1$.

pli-f-559 · level facile · notes
The feasible region of an ILP problem is $F = \{(0,0), (1,0), (0,1)\}$. Which formulation is ideal?
  1. $x_1 + x_2 \le 1$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$.
  2. $2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 3$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$.
  3. $x_1 \le 1$, $x_2 \le 1$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$.
  4. $x_1 + x_2 \le 2$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$.

pli-f-560 · level facile · notes
For the region $F = \{(0,0), (1,0), (0,1)\}$, the formulations $P_1: x_1 + x_2 \le 1$ and $P_2: 2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 3$ (with $x \ge 0$) have the same integer points. Which one is stronger?
  1. $P_2$, because it has larger coefficients.
  2. They are equivalent.
  3. $P_1$, because $P_1 \subseteq P_2$.
  4. Neither is a formulation of $F$.
Explanation. Every point with $x_1 + x_2 \le 1$ satisfies $2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 2 \le 3$; not conversely.

4. Valid inequalities (32)

pli-f-040 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region (set of feasible integer solutions) of an ILP problem and $P$ the polyhedron of a formulation of it. When is the inequality $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \beta$ valid for the problem?
  1. When it is satisfied by all the points of the polyhedron $P$.
  2. When it cuts off the optimal solution of the relaxation.
  3. When it is satisfied by all the points of $F$.
  4. When at least one point of $F$ satisfies it with equality.
Explanation. Valid means that it cuts off no feasible integer solution; it can cut off fractional points of $P$. The last option describes a tight inequality.
pli-f-041 · level facile · notes
Let $F$ be the feasible region of an ILP problem. An inequality valid for $F$ (satisfied by all the points of $F$) is called tight when:
  1. at least one point of $F$ satisfies it with equality.
  2. all the points of $F$ satisfy it with equality.
  3. it cuts off the optimal solution of the LP relaxation.
  4. its right-hand side is integer.
Explanation. The right-hand side of a tight inequality cannot be decreased without losing a feasible solution.
pli-f-042 · level facile · notes
What is the effect of adding a valid inequality (satisfied by all the feasible integer solutions $F$) to a formulation of a maximization ILP problem?
  1. It reduces $F$ by removing integer solutions, and can improve the optimal value of the ILP problem.
  2. $F$ does not change, the polyhedron shrinks and the upper bound of the relaxation does not get worse.
  3. It always improves the rounded upper bound of the relaxation, even if it does not cut off the LP optimum.
  4. It makes the formulation ideal, that is the polyhedron equals $\mathrm{conv}(F)$.
Explanation. A valid inequality removes no integer point, so the new formulation is stronger; but the upper bound improves only if the optimum of the relaxation is cut off.
pli-f-043 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the first constraint is multiplied by $\frac{1}{2}$ and, since the left-hand side is integer for every integer solution, the right-hand side is rounded down. Which valid inequality is obtained?
  1. $5x_1 + 2x_2 \le 23$
  2. $5x_1 + 2x_2 \le 21$
  3. $5x_1 + 4x_2 \le 22$
  4. $5x_1 + 2x_2 \le 22$
Explanation. $5x_1 + 2x_2 \le \frac{45}{2}$ with an integer left-hand side: the right-hand side is rounded down to $22$.
pli-f-044 · level facile · notes
In a MIP problem the variable $x_2$ is continuous and the others are integer. The $\le$ constraints are combined with nonnegative multipliers. Can the right-hand side of the combination be rounded down, if $x_2$ has a nonzero coefficient in it?
  1. No: the left-hand side is not necessarily integer.
  2. Yes, provided that the multipliers are non-negative.
  3. Yes, provided that the coefficients are integer.
  4. Yes, but rounding up.
Explanation. Rounding is allowed only if the left-hand side is integer for every feasible solution, that is with integer variables and integer coefficients; with $x_2$ continuous this fails.
pli-f-045 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$), the inequality $x_1 + x_2 \le 6$ is valid. What does it do with respect to the optimum of the LP relaxation $(\frac{7}{2}, \frac{5}{2})$?
  1. It cuts it off and brings the rounded upper bound to $14$.
  2. It does not cut it off, because at that point $x_1 + x_2$ equals exactly $6$.
  3. It cuts it off, but the rounded upper bound stays $15$.
  4. It is not valid, because it cuts off the integer solution $(4,1)$.
Explanation. $\frac{7}{2} + \frac{5}{2} = 6$: the point satisfies the inequality with equality. The tight inequality $x_1 + x_2 \le 5$ instead cuts it off; stopping at a loose right-hand side is a typical mistake.
pli-f-046 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the LP relaxation has optimal value $z(\LP) = \frac{31}{2}$ and rounded upper bound $\lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor = 15$. The valid inequalities $5x_1 + 2x_2 \le 22$ and $2x_1 + 7x_2 \le 24$ both cut off the optimum $(\frac{7}{2}, \frac{5}{2})$. What does the rounded upper bound become when one of them is added?
  1. It drops to $14$ with either of them.
  2. It drops to $13$ with either of them.
  3. It stays $15$ with either of them.
  4. It rises to $16$ with either of them.
Explanation. The new values are $\frac{472}{31}$ and $\frac{957}{62}$, both with integer part $15$: an inequality that cuts off the LP optimum does not always improve the rounded bound.
pli-f-047 · level facile · notes
Combining the constraints of an ILP problem with multipliers $\pi_i \ge 0$ gives the valid inequality $2x_1 + 3x_2 \le \frac{37}{3}$, with $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$. What is the smallest right-hand side obtainable from this inequality alone by exploiting integrality?
  1. $2x_1 + 3x_2 \le 13$
  2. $2x_1 + 3x_2 \le \frac{38}{3}$
  3. $2x_1 + 3x_2 \le 11$
  4. $2x_1 + 3x_2 \le 12$
Explanation. The left-hand side is integer, so the right-hand side is rounded down: $\lfloor \frac{37}{3} \rfloor = 12$. Rounding up ($13$) is weaker; $11$ does not follow from this inequality.
pli-f-048 · level media · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$), which inequality with coefficients $(1, 2)$ is valid and tight, that is satisfied by all the feasible integer points and with equality by at least one?
  1. $x_1 + 2x_2 \le 7$
  2. $x_1 + 2x_2 \le 8$
  3. $x_1 + 2x_2 \le 6$
  4. $x_1 + 2x_2 \le \frac{17}{2}$
Explanation. With $\beta = 8$ we get $x_1 = 8 - 2x_2$: the constraints give $x_2 \ge 3$ and $x_2 \le 2$, a contradiction. With $\beta = 7$ the points $(3,2)$ and $(1,3)$ are feasible: $x_1 + 2x_2 \le 7$ is tight.
pli-f-049 · level teorema · notes
Consider an ILP problem with constraints $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j \le b_i$ and $\vet x \in \Z^n_{\ge 0}$, and multipliers $\pi_i$. Which statement about the valid inequalities obtained by rounding is correct?
  1. For multipliers $\pi_i$ of any sign, $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \lfloor \sum_i \pi_i b_i \rfloor$ is valid.
  2. If $\pi_i \ge 0$, $\sum_i \pi_i a_{ij} \ge \alpha_j$ and $\alpha_j \in \Z$ for every $j$, then $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \lfloor \sum_i \pi_i b_i \rfloor$ is valid.
  3. If $\pi_i \ge 0$ and $\sum_i \pi_i a_{ij} \ge \alpha_j$, then $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \lceil \sum_i \pi_i b_i \rceil$ is valid and tight.
  4. The rounded inequality is valid even if some variables are continuous.
Explanation. Non-negative multipliers, integer coefficients bounded by the combination and integer variables are needed; the right-hand side is rounded down.
pli-f-050 · level teorema · notes
Let $F$ be the set of feasible integer solutions of an ILP problem and $P$ the polyhedron of a formulation of it. Which statement about valid inequalities is correct?
  1. Every inequality valid for $F$ is valid for $P$.
  2. Every valid inequality is tight.
  3. An inequality is valid for $F$ if and only if it is valid for $\mathrm{conv}(F)$.
  4. An inequality valid for $\mathrm{conv}(F)$ can cut off a point of $F$.
Explanation. A half-space containing $F$ also contains its convex combinations; the inequalities of the ideal formulation are valid.
pli-f-051 · level ipotesi · notes
For an ILP problem with constraints $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j \le b_i$ and $\vet x \in \Z^n_{\ge 0}$, we want to prove that if $\pi_i \ge 0$, $\sum_i \pi_i a_{ij} \ge \alpha_j$ and $\alpha_j \in \Z$, then $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \lfloor \sum_i \pi_i b_i \rfloor$ is valid. Which facts are needed?
  1. Strong duality applied to the LP relaxation of the problem.
  2. That the optimum of the LP relaxation is a fractional vertex.
  3. $b_i \ge 0$ and $\alpha_j \ge 0$ for every $i$ and $j$, together with $\pi_i \ge 0$.
  4. $\vet x \ge \vet 0$ and $\pi_i \ge 0$ to bound from above, $x_j$ and $\alpha_j$ integer to round.
Explanation. $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \sum_j (\sum_i \pi_i a_{ij}) x_j \le \sum_i \pi_i b_i$; since the left-hand side is integer, it does not exceed the integer part of the right-hand side.
pli-f-052 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that adding a valid inequality (satisfied by all the feasible integer solutions) to a formulation of a maximization ILP problem does not worsen the upper bound of the LP relaxation, what is needed?
  1. That the integer solutions do not change and the maximum over a smaller set does not grow.
  2. That the inequality is tight for at least one feasible solution.
  3. That the inequality cuts off the optimal solution of the relaxation.
  4. That the new relaxation is re-optimized with the dual simplex.
Explanation. The new formulation is stronger (same $F$, smaller polyhedron); for the mere non-worsening neither tightness nor cutting off the optimum is needed.
pli-f-147 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) we look for the strongest valid inequality $x_1 + x_2 \le \beta$, lowering $\beta$ until a feasible integer point has $x_1 + x_2 = \beta$. With $\beta = 6$ and $x_2 = 6 - x_1$ the constraints give $x_1 \le \frac{7}{2}$ and $x_1 \ge \frac{7}{2}$. What is done?
  1. We stop: $x_1 = \frac{7}{2}$ satisfies both, so $\beta = 6$ is tight.
  2. We set $\beta = 7$: the two bounds coincide, so $\beta$ must be increased.
  3. We stop with $\beta = 6$, because the two bounds do not contradict each other.
  4. We set $\beta = 5$: for integer $x_1$ we need $x_1 \le 3$ and $x_1 \ge 4$, which contradict each other.
Explanation. The point must be integer: $x_1 \le \lfloor \frac{7}{2} \rfloor = 3$ and $x_1 \ge \lceil \frac{7}{2} \rceil = 4$. No integer point has sum $6$; with $\beta = 5$ we find $(3,2)$ and $(4,1)$.
pli-f-148 · level facile · notes
With many variables the valid inequalities of an ILP problem are not chosen on a picture. Where do they come from in general?
  1. From families for special structures (covers) and from rounding dictionary rows (Gomory).
  2. Only from the ideal formulation, computed once and for all from the hull.
  3. From rounding the components of the optimal solution of the relaxation.
  4. From the upper bounds on the variables, which are always enough to get the convex hull.
Explanation. Cover inequalities exploit a constraint with positive coefficients of a binary problem; Gomory fractional cuts apply the rounding systematically to the rows of the optimal dictionary.
pli-f-149 · level facile · notes
For the constraint $5x_1 + 4x_2 + 3x_3 \le 8$ with $x_1, x_2, x_3 \in \{0,1\}$, the inequality $x_1 + x_2 \le 1$ is valid because $5 + 4 > 8$. Which family does it belong to?

  2. To the upper bounds on the variables $x_j \le \lfloor b / a_j \rfloor$.
  3. To the cover inequalities of the constraint.
  4. To the inequalities of the ideal formulation of the car example.
Explanation. $\{1,2\}$ is a cover (coefficients summing to more than $8$): at most $|C| - 1 = 1$ of its variables equals $1$.
pli-f-150 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) a student uses the multipliers $(\frac{1}{20}, \frac{1}{20})$: he gets $\frac{7}{10}x_1 + \frac{9}{10}x_2 \le \frac{47}{10}$ and writes $x_1 + x_2 \le 4$. What is wrong?
  1. Nothing: it is valid and stronger than $x_1 + x_2 \le 5$, since $\frac{47}{10}$ rounds down to $4$.
  2. The right-hand side should have been rounded up, to $5$, because the coefficients are below $1$.
  3. The multipliers must be integer for the right-hand side to be rounded.
  4. The combined coefficients are below $(1, 1)$: the rounding does not apply, and $(4,1)$ violates it.
Explanation. To write $x_1 + x_2 \le \lfloor \cdot \rfloor$ the combined coefficients must be at least $1$; here $\frac{7}{10}$ and $\frac{9}{10}$ are not enough. The feasible point $(4,1)$ has sum $5 > 4$.
pli-f-151 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the feasible solution $(4,1)$ of value $14$ is known. With the valid inequality $x_1 + x_2 \le 5$ the relaxation has the optimum $(\frac{25}{6}, \frac{5}{6})$, of value $\frac{85}{6}$. What is concluded?
  1. Nothing: the optimum of the relaxation is fractional, so more is needed.
  2. $\lfloor \frac{85}{6} \rfloor = 14$: the upper bound equals $14$ and $(4,1)$ is certified optimal.
  3. The upper bound is $15$, because $\frac{85}{6}$ is rounded up.
  4. $(4,1)$ is optimal only if $x_1 + 2x_2 \le 7$ is added as well.
Explanation. With integer variables and costs $z(\ILP) \le \lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor = 14$; together with $z(\ILP) \ge 14$ the gap is zero.
pli-f-152 · level facile · notes
For the car example ($10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the valid inequalities $x_1 \le 4$, $x_2 \le 3$, $x_1 + x_2 \le 5$, $x_1 + 2x_2 \le 7$ were derived. Which polyhedron do they describe, with $\vet x \ge \vet 0$?

pli-f-266 · level facile · notes

pli-f-267 · level teorema · notes
In a canonical ILP problem $\mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b$, $\vet x \in \Z^n_{\ge 0}$, what does the proposition on valid inequalities by rounding state?
  1. If $\pi_i \ge 0$ and $\sum_i \pi_i a_{ij} \le \alpha_j$ for every $j$, then $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \lfloor \sum_i \pi_i b_i \rfloor$ is valid.
  2. If $\pi_i \ge 0$ and $\alpha_j$ real with $\sum_i \pi_i a_{ij} \ge \alpha_j$, then $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \lfloor \sum_i \pi_i b_i \rfloor$ is valid.
  3. If $\pi_i \ge 0$, $\alpha_j$ integer and $\sum_i \pi_i a_{ij} \ge \alpha_j$, then $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \lceil \sum_i \pi_i b_i \rceil - 1$ is valid.
  4. If $\pi_i \ge 0$, $\alpha_j$ integer and $\sum_i \pi_i a_{ij} \ge \alpha_j$ for every $j$, then $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \lfloor \sum_i \pi_i b_i \rfloor$ is valid.
Explanation. A nonnegative combination, then integer coefficients and integer variables allow rounding the right-hand side down.
pli-f-268 · level facile · notes
For an ILP problem with feasible region $F$, when is a valid inequality $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \beta$ tight?
  1. When it cuts off the optimal solution of the LP relaxation.
  2. When at least one point of $F$ satisfies it with equality.
  3. When all points of $F$ satisfy it with equality.
  4. When its right-hand side is integer.
Explanation. The right-hand side of a tight inequality cannot be lowered without losing a feasible point.
pli-f-269 · level teorema · notes
For an ILP problem with finite feasible region $F \neq \emptyset$ and fixed coefficients $\alpha$, which right-hand side makes $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \beta$ valid and tight?
  1. $\beta = \max\{\sum_j \alpha_j x_j : \vet x \in P\}$, over the relaxation polyhedron.
  2. $\beta = \max\{\sum_j \alpha_j x_j : \vet x \in F\}$.
  3. $\beta = \lfloor \max\{\sum_j \alpha_j x_j : \vet x \in P\} \rfloor$, in every case.
  4. $\beta = \min\{\sum_j \alpha_j x_j : \vet x \in F\}$.
Explanation. Valid requires $\beta \ge$ the maximum over $F$; tight requires some point to reach it: so $\beta$ is that maximum.
pli-f-270 · level facile · notes
In the algorithm that improves a valid inequality $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \beta$ of an ILP problem, what is done at each step?
  1. Increase $\beta$ by $1$ until the inequality becomes valid.
  2. Change the coefficients $\alpha_j$ until the inequality cuts off the fractional optimum.
  3. Divide everything by the greatest common divisor of the coefficients.
  4. If no feasible integer point satisfies $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j = \beta$, set $\beta \leftarrow \beta - 1$ and repeat.
Explanation. Start from a valid right-hand side (Step 0) and lower it until a point of $F$ reaches it: the inequality is then tight.
pli-f-271 · level facile · notes
For the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the inequality $x_1 + x_2 \le 6$ is valid. Lowering the right-hand side until a feasible integer point reaches it, which tight inequality is obtained?

  2. $x_1 + x_2 \le 4$.
  3. $x_1 + x_2 \le 6$.
  4. $x_1 + x_2 \le 3$.
Explanation. No point of $F$ has $x_1 + x_2 = 6$; the maximum of $x_1 + x_2$ over $F$ is $5$, e.g. at $(3,2)$.
pli-f-272 · level ipotesi · notes
The algorithm that lowers by $1$ at a time the right-hand side $\beta$ of a valid inequality $\sum_j \alpha_j x_j \le \beta$ returns a tight inequality. Which assumptions are needed?
  1. That the LP relaxation has an integer optimum.
  2. That all coefficients $\alpha_j$ are positive.
  3. $F \neq \emptyset$ and integer coefficients $\alpha_j$.
  4. That the problem has exactly two variables.
Explanation. With integer values, stepping down by $1$ does not skip the maximum over $F$; with $F$ empty the algorithm would not stop.
pli-f-273 · level facile · notes
From which two sources do the valid inequalities used in integer linear programming come, in general?
  1. From the dual problem and from heuristic solutions.
  2. From the variable upper bounds and from the objective function.
  3. From the picture of the problem, in any dimension.
  4. Families for specific constraint structures.
Explanation. With two variables we look at pictures; in general we use structural families or systematic rounding.
pli-f-274 · level facile · notes
In an ILP problem, what is meant by a cut with respect to a fractional point $\vet x^*$ of the LP relaxation?
  1. A constraint that removes the optimal integer solution.
  2. An inequality satisfied by $\vet x^*$ with equality.
  3. A valid inequality violated by $\vet x^*$.
  4. An upper bound on the optimal value.
Explanation. A cut separates $\vet x^*$ from $F$: it is satisfied by all integer points and violated by $\vet x^*$.
pli-f-275 · level facile · notes
Combining the constraints of an ILP problem with nonnegative multipliers yields $x_1 + 2x_2 \le 7.5$. Which inequality can be derived by rounding?

pli-f-276 · level facile · notes
A MIP problem has $x_1$ integer and $x_2$ continuous; a combination of the constraints gives $x_1 + x_2 \le 3.5$. Can we write $x_1 + x_2 \le 3$?
  1. Yes: it is enough that one variable is integer.

pli-f-277 · level facile · notes
A valid inequality that cuts off the fractional optimum of the LP relaxation of a maximization ILP problem is added. What happens to the upper bound $z(\LP)$?

  2. It becomes equal to $z(\ILP)$.
  3. It does not increase, but it can stay the same.
