  3. $\frac{4}{41} \approx 9.8\%$.
  4. $\frac{4}{37} \approx 10.8\%$.
Explanation. $\UB = \max\{40, 38, 41\} = 41$, gap $= (41 - 37)/37$.
pli-f-418 · level ipotesi · notes
In a branch-and-cut algorithm for a maximization ILP problem with integer data, to prove that $\UB = \max_{k \in L} \mathrm{UB}(\LP_k)$ computed after the cuts is still a valid upper bound, what is needed?
  1. That all cuts are global.
  2. That the cuts are selected by efficacy.
  3. That every cut used at node $k$ is valid for $F_k$.
  4. That the cut pool is empty.
Explanation. Local validity is enough: the node keeps its integer solutions.
pli-f-419 · level facile · notes
In a branch-and-cut algorithm for a maximization ILP problem with integer data, a student adds to the global pool a Gomory cut generated at node $5$ (with branching constraints). Why is this risky?
  1. The cut may depend on the branching constraints.
  2. It is not risky: Gomory cuts are always global.
  3. Because the global pool accepts only cover cuts.
  4. Because node $5$ will be pruned.
Explanation. The algorithm keeps a cut generated at a node local, unless it is proved not to use branching constraints.
pli-f-420 · level facile · notes
In a branch-and-cut algorithm for a maximization ILP problem with integer data, why is it not worth adding all violated cuts found in a round?
  1. Because violated cuts are not valid.
  2. They make the LP problems large and slow.
  3. Because they increase the upper bound.
  4. Because they make the problem infeasible.
Explanation. Cuts are selected by efficacy and inactive ones are removed.
pli-f-421 · level facile · notes
In a branch-and-cut algorithm for a maximization ILP problem with integer data, after adding a cut at a node, how is the LP problem re-optimized?
  1. With the primal simplex from scratch.
  2. With the graphical method.
  3. With the dual simplex, starting from the previous dictionary.
  4. It is not re-optimized: branching starts immediately.
Explanation. The dictionary stays dual feasible but loses primal feasibility.
pli-f-422 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the effect of cuts in a branch-and-cut algorithm is correct?
  1. Effective cuts reduce the size of the tree.
  2. Cuts always reduce the total solution time.
  3. Cuts always increase the number of nodes.
  4. Cuts make branching unnecessary.
Explanation. On problem $P10$ a Gomory cut reduces the tree; too many cuts slow things down.
pli-f-569 · level ipotesi · notes
A branch-and-cut algorithm remains an exact method. Which condition on the cuts added at a node $k$ guarantees it?
  1. That they are all global.
  2. That they are valid for $F_k$.
  3. That they are Gomory cuts.
  4. That they make the node solution integer.
Explanation. With cuts valid at the node no solution of its region is lost.

Part: MIP solvers (85)

9. MIP solvers: what they do (85)

pli-f-105 · level facile · notes
In a maximization problem, what do the columns $\texttt{Incumbent}$ and $\texttt{BestBd}$ of the Gurobi log report?
  1. $\texttt{Incumbent}$ is the lower bound (primal side), $\texttt{BestBd}$ the upper bound (dual side).
  2. $\texttt{Incumbent}$ is the upper bound (dual side), $\texttt{BestBd}$ the lower bound (primal side).
  3. Both are upper bounds: the incumbent of the root, the best bound of the open nodes.
  4. $\texttt{BestBd}$ is the value of the relaxation of the current node, not a global bound.
Explanation. The incumbent is the best feasible solution; the best bound is the maximum of the bounds of the open nodes. The value of the current node is in the column $\texttt{Obj}$.
pli-f-106 · level facile · notes
The Gurobi log of a <u>minimization</u> problem whose objective takes integer values on the feasible solutions reports $\texttt{Incumbent} = 812$ and $\texttt{BestBd} = 795.3$. What are the best bounds on the optimal value $z$?
  1. $795 \le z \le 812$
  2. $796 \le z \le 812$
  3. $796 \le z \le 811$
  4. $812$ is a lower bound and $796$ an upper bound.
Explanation. In minimization $\texttt{Incumbent}$ is the upper bound and $\texttt{BestBd}$ the lower bound; with integer values the lower bound is rounded up: $\lceil 795.3 \rceil = 796$. $795$ is valid but weaker, and $811$ is not guaranteed.
pli-f-107 · level facile · notes
At the end of a Gurobi run on a MIP the log reports the message $\texttt{Optimal solution found (tolerance 1.00e-04)}$. What does it mean?
  1. That the solution is optimal, with certainty.
  2. That the whole tree has been explored.
  3. That the incumbent is optimal up to a relative gap of $10^{-4}$.
  4. That the solution violates the constraints by at most $10^{-4}$.
Explanation. The solver stops when the gap falls below $\texttt{MIPGap}$; with a larger tolerance it writes the same sentence for a solution that may not be optimal.
pli-f-108 · level facile · notes
What does the Gurobi parameter $\texttt{IntFeasTol}$ (default $10^{-5}$) control?
  1. The relative gap between incumbent and best bound at which the solver stops.
  2. The maximum allowed violation of a linear constraint.
  3. The tolerance on the reduced costs in the optimality test of the simplex.
  4. How far a variable can be from an integer to be considered integer.
Explanation. The solver works in floating point: the gap is $\texttt{MIPGap}$, the constraint violation $\texttt{FeasibilityTol}$, the reduced costs $\texttt{OptimalityTol}$.
pli-f-109 · level facile · notes
What does the presolve of a MIP solver do?
  1. It transforms it into an equivalent problem, smaller and often with a stronger relaxation.
  2. It finds a first feasible solution with a heuristic, before the root node.
  3. It solves the LP relaxation of the root node with the dual simplex.
  4. It chooses in advance the branching variable for every node of the tree.
Explanation. The typical operations are removing redundant constraints and fixing variables, bound tightening and coefficient tightening.
pli-f-110 · level facile · notes
With $x_1, x_2, x_3 \in \{0,1\}$, the constraint $6x_1 + 6x_2 + 3x_3 \le 10$ has the same feasible binary points as which constraint with a stronger relaxation?
  1. $6x_1 + 6x_2 + 3x_3 \le 9$
  2. $7x_1 + 7x_2 + 3x_3 \le 10$
  3. $6x_1 + 6x_2 + 4x_3 \le 10$
  4. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 1$
Explanation. Only $x_1 = x_2 = 1$ violates both; the other points have left-hand sides at most $9$ and $10$. The relaxation decreases from $\frac{13}{6}$ to $2$.
pli-f-111 · level facile · notes
In the Gurobi log, what does a line starting with $\texttt{H}$ and one starting with $\texttt{*}$ indicate?
  1. $\texttt{H}$: a node pruned by bound; $\texttt{*}$: a cut added at the current node.
  2. $\texttt{H}$: a new best bound from the open nodes; $\texttt{*}$: a new incumbent from a heuristic.
  3. $\texttt{H}$: a new incumbent from a heuristic; $\texttt{*}$: a new incumbent from the integer solution of a node.
  4. $\texttt{H}$: the end of presolve; $\texttt{*}$: the end of the processing of the root node.
Explanation. Primal heuristics give the incumbent; the asterisk corresponds to pruning a node by integrality.
pli-f-112 · level facile · notes
In the choice of the branching variable of a MIP solver, strong branching evaluates the candidates by solving the relaxations of the children; pseudocosts estimate the same effect from past branchings. What is reliability branching?
  1. Strong branching applied to all the fractional variables at every node of the tree.
  2. The choice of the fractional variable with minimum index, the easiest to reproduce.
  3. Maximum infeasibility, with ties broken at random to avoid cycles.
  4. Strong branching on a variable while its pseudocosts are unreliable, then pseudocosts.
Explanation. Strong branching gives small trees but is expensive; pseudocosts are cheap but unreliable at the beginning.
pli-f-113 · level media · notes
A maximization problem with integer data stops with $\texttt{Incumbent} = 593$ and best bound $597$; $\texttt{MIPGap} = 0.01$ and Gurobi computes the gap as $|\text{bound} - \text{incumbent}| / |\text{incumbent}|$. What is the gap, and what can be said about the solution?
  1. $\approx 0.67\%$: the solution is within $0.67\%$ of the optimum, but it is not certified optimal.
  2. $\approx 0.67\%$: the solution is certified optimal, because the gap is below $\texttt{MIPGap}$.
  3. $\approx 0.67\%$ computed on $597$, the best bound: the solver continues.
  4. $4\%$, the absolute difference: the solver continues until a zero gap.
Explanation. $\frac{597 - 593}{593} \approx 0.67\% < 1\%$: the solver stops and writes the same sentence as for the optimum, but without exploring the remaining nodes it cannot know whether $593$ is optimal.
pli-f-114 · level teorema · notes
Which statement on presolve is correct?
  1. Presolve can change the optimal value, but it always improves it by removing redundant constraints.

  3. Presolve only removes fixed variables, without touching constraints and coefficients.
  4. Presolve is a primal heuristic that gives the first incumbent of the solver.
Explanation. The problem after presolve is equivalent; coefficient tightening, for example, shrinks the relaxation without changing the integer solutions.

  4. The gap is computed dividing by the best bound, and the solver stops when it is zero.

pli-f-188 · level facile · notes
In which order does a MIP solver (Gurobi, CPLEX, HiGHS, …) execute its main phases?

  4. Root node without cuts, then presolve, then cuts only at the leaves.
Explanation. The core is branch-and-cut; presolve simplifies the model before the root, and the solver stops when the gap between the bounds falls below the tolerance or a limit is reached.

pli-f-519 · level ipotesi · notes
In the generic MIP $G_1$ ($x_j \in \{0,1,2,3\}$, $a_{ij} \ge 1$, $b_i = \sum_j a_{ij}$), to prove that the point with all $x_j = 1$ is feasible, what is needed?
  1. That the costs $c_j$ are positive.
  2. That the LP relaxation has an integer optimum.
  3. That presolve is active.
  4. Only the definition $b_i = \sum_j a_{ij}$.
Explanation. $\sum_j a_{ij} \cdot 1 = b_i$ for every $i$, and $1 \in \{0,1,2,3\}$.
pli-f-520 · level facile · notes

  4. The maximum number of nodes.
Explanation. With the default value the solution is optimal up to $0.01\%$.
pli-f-521 · level facile · notes

pli-f-522 · level facile · notes
In Gurobi, what is the difference between MIPGap and MIPGapAbs?

  2. MIPGapAbs is the integrality tolerance.
  3. They are two names for the same parameter.
  4. MIPGap is a relative gap, MIPGapAbs an absolute gap between incumbent and best bound.
Explanation. The solver stops when either gap drops below its threshold.
pli-f-523 · level facile · notes
What is meant by coefficient tightening in the presolve of a MIP solver?

  4. Rounding all coefficients up.

Which of the following is not a typical presolve operation of a MIP solver?

Part: MIP heuristics (92)

10. MIP heuristics (92)

pli-f-118 · level facile · notes
What kind of bound on the optimal value does a heuristic (even ILP-based) give?
  1. A dual bound: an upper bound in maximization.
  2. A primal bound: a lower bound in maximization.
  3. Both, if the heuristic uses the LP relaxation.
  4. No bound, because the solution may not be optimal.
Explanation. The heuristic returns a feasible solution; its quality is measured only with a dual bound, for example the one of the solver's branch-and-bound.
pli-f-119 · level facile · notes
Rounding a value $v$ to the nearest integer is defined as $\lfloor v + \frac{1}{2} \rfloor$. What is its value for $v = \frac{5}{2}$?
  1. $2$
  2. $2.5$
  3. $3$
  4. It depends on the variable
Explanation. $\lfloor \frac{5}{2} + \frac{1}{2} \rfloor = 3$: the value $\frac{1}{2}$ is rounded up.
pli-f-120 · level facile · notes
Let $\mathcal{L} = \{\vet x \in \R^n : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet l \le \vet x \le \vet u\}$ be the region of the LP relaxation of a MIP, and let $\lfloor \tilde{\vet x} \rfloor$ be the point obtained from $\tilde{\vet x}$ by rounding the integer variables down. All coefficients of $\mat A$ are nonnegative, $\vet b \ge \vet 0$ and $\vet l = \vet 0$. What can be guaranteed for $\tilde{\vet x} \in \mathcal{L}$?
  1. That $\lfloor \tilde{\vet x} \rfloor$ belongs to $\mathcal{L}$.
  2. That rounding to the nearest integer belongs to $\mathcal{L}$.
  3. That $\lfloor \tilde{\vet x} \rfloor$ is an optimal solution of the MIP.
  4. That rounding up belongs to $\mathcal{L}$.
Explanation. Rounding down does not increase any left-hand side with nonnegative coefficients, and stays $\ge \vet 0$; in the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$) the point obtained is not optimal ($13$ instead of $14$).
pli-f-121 · level facile · notes
A diving heuristic solves the LP relaxation, fixes (or restricts) a fractional variable and repeats. What is the relation between diving and branch-and-bound?
  1. Diving is a complete branch-and-bound with the best bound strategy.
  2. Diving explores the whole tree, visiting it breadth-first.
  3. Diving fixes the variables without solving any LP problem.
  4. Diving follows a single path from the root, without backtracking.
Explanation. Fixing $x_j$ to the rounded value is a branching decision: diving is a depth-first visit without backtracking, and every step re-optimizes the LP problem with the dual simplex.
pli-f-122 · level facile · notes
In the feasibility pump, $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x}) = \sum_j |x_j - \bar x_j|$ is the distance between a point $\vet x \in [0,1]^n$ of the relaxation and a binary point $\bar{\vet x}$. How is it written in linear form in $\vet x$?
  1. $\sum_{j : \bar x_j = 0} x_j + \sum_{j : \bar x_j = 1} (1 - x_j)$
  2. $\sum_{j : \bar x_j = 1} x_j + \sum_{j : \bar x_j = 0} (1 - x_j)$
  3. $\sum_{j : \bar x_j = 0} x_j - \sum_{j : \bar x_j = 1} x_j$
  4. $\sum_{j : \bar x_j = 0} (1 - x_j) + \sum_{j : \bar x_j = 1} (1 - x_j)$
Explanation. For $x_j \in [0,1]$, $|x_j - \bar x_j|$ equals $x_j$ if $\bar x_j = 0$ and $1 - x_j$ if $\bar x_j = 1$: the distance is linear in $\vet x$.
pli-f-123 · level facile · notes
The RINS heuristic uses the incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$ and the solution $\tilde{\vet x}$ of the LP relaxation of a node, and solves a sub-MIP with some variables fixed. Which variables does it fix?
  1. All the integer variables, at the values of the solution $\tilde{\vet x}$ rounded.
  2. The integer variables on which $\bar{\vet x}$ and $\tilde{\vet x}$ agree, at the common value.
  3. A number $k$ of integer variables chosen at random, at the values of $\bar{\vet x}$.
  4. The integer variables on which $\bar{\vet x}$ and $\tilde{\vet x}$ differ, at the values of $\bar{\vet x}$.
Explanation. Where the two «advisers» agree the value is probably right; the sub-MIP decides the rest. It is hard fixing.
pli-f-124 · level facile · notes
Local branching around a binary point $\bar{\vet x}$ adds the constraint $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x}) \le k$, where $\Delta$ counts the binary variables in which $\vet x$ differs from $\bar{\vet x}$. With $k = 1$, which binary points does the neighbourhood contain?
  1. Only $\bar{\vet x}$.
  2. All the binary points.
  3. $\bar{\vet x}$ and the points obtained by changing the value of a single variable.
  4. The points that differ from $\bar{\vet x}$ in exactly two variables.
Explanation. The constraint counts the variables that change: with $k = 1$ at most one can change, so the neighbourhood has $1 + n$ binary points ($n$ binary variables).
pli-f-125 · level facile · notes
In relax-and-fix the integer variables of a MIP are split into blocks $B_1, \dots, B_p$ and at step $r = 1, \dots, p$ a MIP is solved. At step $r$, how are the variables of the blocks treated?
  1. All integer, but with only the constraints that contain $B_r$.
  2. $B_r$ continuous, $B_1, \dots, B_{r-1}$ fixed, the others integer.
  3. $B_1, \dots, B_{r-1}$ free, $B_r$ fixed, $B_{r+1}, \dots, B_p$ integer.
  4. $B_1, \dots, B_{r-1}$ fixed, $B_r$ integer, $B_{r+1}, \dots, B_p$ continuous.
Explanation. Each MIP has the integer variables of one block only, so it is small; the relaxed blocks are optimistic (myopia).
pli-f-126 · level media · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) fractional diving is applied: the fractional variable closest to an integer is fixed to the nearest integer (ties by minimum index, $\frac{1}{2}$ rounded up) and the relaxation is solved again, starting from its optimum $(\frac{7}{2}, \frac{5}{2})$. What does it return?
  1. $(4,1)$, of value $14$
  2. $(3,2)$, of value $13$
  3. $(4,3)$, of value $18$
  4. It fails: the LP problem becomes infeasible
Explanation. Tie at distance $\frac{1}{2}$: $x_1 = 4$ is fixed; the new optimum is $(4, \frac{5}{4})$, $x_2 = 1$ is fixed and the solution $(4,1)$ is integer, and it is the optimum of the problem.
pli-f-127 · level teorema · notes
In proximity search for a MIP $\min f(\vet x)$ with incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$, the proximity sub-MIP minimizes the distance $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x})$ over the feasible solutions with the constraint $f(\vet x) \le f(\bar{\vet x}) - \theta$, where $\theta > 0$. Which statement is correct?
  1. If the proximity sub-MIP is infeasible for any $\theta > 0$, even with non-integer $f$, the incumbent is optimal.
  2. If $f$ is integer on all the feasible solutions and the sub-MIP with $\theta = 1$ is infeasible, $\bar{\vet x}$ is optimal.
  3. If the local branching sub-MIP with $k = 1$ around $\bar{\vet x}$ is infeasible, the incumbent is optimal.
  4. If RINS, with the variables on which LP and incumbent agree fixed, finds no better solution, the incumbent is optimal.
Explanation. With $\theta = 1$ and integer values the constraint $f(\vet x) \le f(\bar{\vet x}) - 1$ excludes exactly the solutions not better than $\bar{\vet x}$: if nothing is left, $\bar{\vet x}$ is optimal.
pli-f-128 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about ILP-based heuristics (diving, feasibility pump, RINS, local branching) is correct?
  1. A heuristic gives a dual bound on the optimal value of the problem.
  2. The feasibility pump always returns an optimal solution.
  3. Inside a solver its solution has a known gap, and a zero gap certifies optimality.
  4. Diving always finds a feasible solution, if one exists.
Explanation. Inside a solver the dual bound of branch-and-bound is available. The feasibility pump only looks for feasibility, and diving can fail, because it fixes variables without backtracking.
pli-f-129 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\mathcal{L} = \{\vet x \in \R^n : \mat A\,\vet x \le \vet b,\ \vet l \le \vet x \le \vet u\}$ be the region of the LP relaxation of a MIP, and let $\lfloor \tilde{\vet x} \rfloor$ be the point obtained from $\tilde{\vet x}$ by rounding the integer variables down. To prove that, if $\tilde{\vet x} \in \mathcal{L}$, also $\lfloor \tilde{\vet x} \rfloor \in \mathcal{L}$, what is needed?
  1. $\vet b \le \vet 0$ and $\vet l = \vet 0$.
  2. That $\tilde{\vet x}$ is an optimal vertex of the relaxation.
  3. That $\vet b$ and $\vet u$ are integer.
  4. $a_{ij} \ge 0$, so that no left-hand side increases, and $\vet l = \vet 0$.
Explanation. Row by row $\sum_j a_{ij} \lfloor \tilde x_j \rfloor \le \sum_j a_{ij} \tilde x_j \le b_i$; moreover $0 \le \lfloor \tilde x_j \rfloor \le \tilde x_j \le u_j$ because $\tilde x_j \ge l_j = 0$.
pli-f-130 · level ipotesi · notes
In proximity search for a MIP $\min f(\vet x)$ with incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$, the proximity sub-MIP minimizes the distance $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x})$ over the feasible solutions with the constraint $f(\vet x) \le f(\bar{\vet x}) - \theta$, where $\theta > 0$. To prove that, if $f$ is integer on all the feasible solutions and the sub-MIP with $\theta = 1$ is infeasible, $\bar{\vet x}$ is optimal, what is needed?
  1. That, with $f$ integer, $f(\vet x) > f(\bar{\vet x}) - 1$ implies $f(\vet x) \ge f(\bar{\vet x})$.
  2. That the distance $\Delta$ is a linear function of $\vet x$.
  3. That the LP relaxation of the sub-MIP has an integer solution.
  4. That the sub-MIP is solved with local branching around $\bar{\vet x}$.
Explanation. The objective $\Delta$ does not matter for infeasibility; what matters is $\theta = 1$ and the integer values of $f$.
pli-f-192 · level facile · notes
For the problem $P7$ ($\max\ 3x_1 + x_2$, $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 12$, $2x_1 + x_2 \le 5$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) the LP relaxation has the optimum $(\frac{5}{2}, 0)$ of value $\frac{15}{2}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. Rounding down gives $(2,0)$, of value $6$, which is optimal.
  2. $(3,0)$, of value $9$, is the optimal solution.
  3. The optimal value is $\frac{15}{2}$, that of the relaxation.
  4. $\lfloor \frac{15}{2} \rfloor = 7$ and $(2,1)$, of value $7$, is feasible: it is optimal.
Explanation. $(2,1)$: $2 + 4 = 6 \le 12$ and $4 + 1 = 5 \le 5$. The bound $7$ is attained, so $(2,1)$ is certified optimal. $(3,0)$ violates $2x_1 + x_2 \le 5$.
pli-f-193 · level facile · notes
In the chapter a MIP has $N$ variables and $\mathscr{D} \subseteq \{1, \dots, N\}$ is the set of the indices of the integer variables. The examples (car, $P7$, $P42$, $P46$, $P72$) are ILP problems in canonical maximization form with integer data. What holds for them?
  1. $\mathscr{D} = \emptyset$, because the heuristics work on the relaxation.
  2. $\mathscr{D} = \{1, \dots, N\}$, and the upper bound is $\lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor$.
  3. $\mathscr{D} = \{1, \dots, N\}$, but the bound is $z(\LP)$ without rounding.
  4. $\mathscr{D}$ is the set of the fractional variables in the LP optimum.
Explanation. All the variables are integer and the objective has integer costs, so it takes integer values on the feasible solutions and the bound is rounded down.
pli-f-194 · level facile · notes
The feasibility pump on the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) rounds the LP optimum $(\frac{7}{2}, \frac{5}{2})$ to $(4,3)$, which is infeasible, and after two flips returns $(3,2)$, of value $13$, while the optimal value is $14$. Why is the solution not optimal?
  1. Because the flips are random and the pump must be repeated with another seed.
  2. Because the $L_1$ distance is not linear on general integer variables.
  3. Because the pump always returns the LP optimum rounded down.
  4. After the first LP the pump ignores the objective: it looks for feasibility only.
Explanation. The projections minimize the distance $\Delta$ from the last integer point, not the objective: the pump finds a first feasible solution, quality is the job of the improvement methods.
pli-f-195 · level facile · notes
Relax-and-fix solves a sequence of MIPs: at step $r$ the variables of block $B_r$ are integer, those of the previous blocks fixed, the later ones relaxed. On $P46$ ($\max\ 5x_1 + 6x_2 + 10x_3$, three constraints, optimum $(0,1,1)$ of value $16$) with blocks $\{x_1\}, \{x_2\}, \{x_3\}$ it gives $(0,2,0)$ of value $12$; with $\{x_1\}, \{x_2, x_3\}$ it gives the optimum. Why?
  1. Because larger blocks relax more variables, and the relaxation is stronger.
  2. With $x_3$ relaxed, $x_2 = 2$ looks convenient; the block $\{x_2, x_3\}$ sees the interaction.
  3. Because with one-variable blocks relax-and-fix finds no feasible solution.
  4. Because relax-and-fix solves only the LP relaxation of each block.
Explanation. The relaxed blocks are optimistic (myopia): once $x_2 = 2$ is fixed, the second constraint leaves room only for $x_3 \le \frac{2}{3}$, that is $x_3 = 0$.
pli-f-196 · level facile · notes
A solution of a MIP computed outside the solver by a heuristic is passed to Gurobi as a MIP start (attribute $\texttt{Start}$ of the variables). What does the solver do with it?
  1. It uses it as initial simplex basis at the root node, and then discards it.
  2. It fixes the variables to those values and optimizes only the continuous variables.
  3. It uses it as initial incumbent, for pruning and for RINS, and never returns anything worse.
  4. It uses it only if it is optimal; otherwise it ignores it.
Explanation. The MIP start gives a primal bound at once: nodes whose bound is not better are pruned, and RINS works around it.
pli-f-197 · level facile · notes
In Gurobi, when are the MIP heuristics run?
  1. Before the root LP, at the root after the LP (rounding, diving, pump), and at some nodes (RINS).
  2. Only at the end of branch-and-bound, to improve the last solution found.
  3. Only before presolve, because afterwards the model changes.
  4. At every node of the tree, all of them, with the same frequency.
Explanation. Heuristics without a relaxation look for a first solution, LP-based ones work at the root, and sub-MIP heuristics use the LP solution of some nodes; the solutions found appear in the log with $\texttt{H}$.
pli-f-198 · level facile · notes
On $P7$ and $P42$ (maximization ILP problems with integer data) the rounded value $\lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor$ of the relaxation already equals the optimal value. What is then the difficulty for a MIP heuristic?
  1. Proving the bound, which requires exploring the whole tree.
  2. None: the rounded LP optimum is always feasible and optimal.
  3. Finding a solution that attains the bound: then the gap is $0$ and the optimum is certified.
  4. The bound is not valid, so the heuristic cannot certify anything.
Explanation. The dual bound is already tight: a solution of value $\lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor$ is enough for a zero gap.
pli-f-199 · level facile · notes
In the Python codes of the chapter, $\texttt{evaluate(m, x\_int)}$ fixes the integer variables of the model $\texttt{m}$ to the values of $\texttt{x\_int}$ and optimizes the continuous ones. When does it return $\texttt{None}$?
  1. When $\texttt{x\_int}$ has fractional components, which cannot be fixed.
  2. When the point cannot be completed: the LP with the integers fixed is infeasible.
  3. When the value obtained is worse than that of the incumbent solution.
  4. When the LP relaxation of the original model is unbounded or infeasible.
Explanation. With the integers fixed an LP in the continuous variables remains: if it has no solution, the integer point cannot be completed and the function returns $\texttt{None}$.
pli-f-200 · level facile · notes
In the codes of the chapter, $\texttt{distance}$ builds in linear form $\Delta(\vet x, \tilde{\vet x}) = \sum_{j \in \mathscr{D}} |x_j - \tilde x_j|$. If $\tilde x_j$ is at its lower bound $l_j$ it uses $x_j - l_j$, if at its upper bound $u_j$ it uses $u_j - x_j$. And otherwise?
  1. The quadratic term $(x_j - \tilde x_j)^2$, which the solver handles.
  2. The term $x_j - \tilde x_j$, ignoring the sign.
  3. No term: the variable does not enter the distance.
  4. An auxiliary variable $d_j$ with $d_j \ge x_j - \tilde x_j$ and $d_j \ge \tilde x_j - x_j$.
Explanation. At the minimum of the distance $d_j = |x_j - \tilde x_j|$; so $\Delta$ stays linear also for general integer variables.
pli-f-201 · level facile · notes
A MIP has the constraint $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = 2$ with $x_j \in \Z_{\ge 0}$, and the relaxation has the solution $(\frac{2}{3}, \frac{2}{3}, \frac{2}{3})$. What does rounding to the nearest integer give?
  1. $(1,1,0)$, feasible, because the rounding respects the constraint.
  2. $(1,1,1)$, infeasible: independent roundings rarely satisfy an equality.
  3. $(0,0,0)$, feasible, because $\frac{2}{3}$ is rounded down.
  4. $(1,1,1)$, feasible, because the constraint is read as $\le$.
Explanation. Every component becomes $1$ and the sum is $3 \neq 2$. With equality constraints rounding fails, and a dive that fixes the variables one at a time is needed.
pli-f-202 · level facile · notes
A sub-MIP heuristic (RINS, local branching) for a MIP $\min f(\vet x)$ with incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$ solves a sub-MIP in a neighbourhood of $\bar{\vet x}$. What is the cutoff constraint $f(\vet x) \le f(\bar{\vet x}) - \theta$, with $\theta > 0$, for?
  1. It makes the sub-MIP smaller, fixing the variables on which LP and incumbent agree.
  2. It guarantees that the sub-MIP always has a solution better than $\bar{\vet x}$.
  3. Without it, the sub-MIP can return $\bar{\vet x}$; with it, an infeasible sub-MIP proves a local optimum.
  4. It makes the distance $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x})$ from the incumbent linear.
Explanation. The cutoff excludes the solutions that are not better by at least $\theta$: if the sub-MIP is infeasible, the neighbourhood contains nothing better.
pli-f-203 · level facile · notes
Among the MIP heuristics of the chapter, which ones need a feasible solution (incumbent) as input?
  1. Rounding, diving and the feasibility pump, which start from the LP solution.
  2. Relax-and-fix and the feasibility pump, which build a solution step by step.
  3. Diving and RINS, but not local branching, which works on the LP.
  4. RINS, local branching, proximity search and fix-and-optimize.
Explanation. Rounding, diving, the pump and relax-and-fix build a solution from the relaxation; the others improve an incumbent with a sub-MIP.
pli-f-204 · level media · notes
A MIP heuristic run inside the solver on the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$) finds $(3,2)$, of value $13$, while the dual bound is $\lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor = 15$. What is the relative gap $(\UB - \LB)/\LB$?
  1. $\frac{2}{13} \approx 15.4\%$
  2. $\frac{2}{15} \approx 13.3\%$
  3. $\frac{1}{14} \approx 7.1\%$
  4. It cannot be computed: a heuristic gives no dual bounds.
Explanation. $\frac{15 - 13}{13} = \frac{2}{13}$. Inside a solver the dual bound is available, so the gap of the heuristic solution is always known.
pli-f-458 · level facile · notes
Consider a MIP $\min\{f(\vet x) : A\,\vet x \le b,\ \vet l \le \vet x \le \vet u,\ x_j \in \Z\ \forall j \in D\}$ with linear $f$ and $D$ the set of integer variables. What is the region $\mathcal{L}$ of its LP relaxation?
  1. $\mathcal{L} = \{\vet x \in \R^N : A\,\vet x \le b,\ \vet l \le \vet x \le \vet u\}$.
  2. $\mathcal{L} = \{\vet x \in \Z^N : A\,\vet x \le b\}$.
  3. $\mathcal{L} = \{\vet x \in \R^N : A\,\vet x \le b\}$, without the bounds on the variables.
  4. The set of feasible solutions of the MIP.
Explanation. Only the integrality constraints are dropped; the bounds $\vet l \le \vet x \le \vet u$ stay.
pli-f-459 · level facile · notes
What characterizes an ILP-based heuristic (matheuristic), compared with a generic constructive heuristic?
  1. It never uses the LP relaxation.
  2. It always guarantees an optimal solution.
  3. It works only on unconstrained problems.
  4. It uses the MIP model and its relaxation.
Explanation. Working on the model, the heuristic also knows a dual bound and hence the gap.
pli-f-460 · level facile · notes
Consider a MIP $\min\{f(\vet x) : A\,\vet x \le b,\ \vet l \le \vet x \le \vet u,\ x_j \in \Z\ \forall j \in D\}$ with linear $f$ and $D$ the set of integer variables. In the chapter on MIP heuristics, what are the rounding of a real value $v$ to the nearest integer and the completion of a point?
  1. The floor and the sum of the continuous variables.
  2. $\lfloor v + \frac12 \rfloor$, and an LP on the continuous ones.
  3. The nearest integer and the removal of the continuous variables.
  4. The ceiling and the computation of the gap.
Explanation. Completion fixes the integer variables and optimizes the continuous ones on the remaining LP.
pli-f-461 · level facile · notes
Problem $P7$ of the chapter on MIP heuristics is $\max\ 3x_1 + x_2$, $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 12$, $2x_1 + x_2 \le 5$, $x \in \Z^2_{\ge 0}$. Which integer solution has value $7$?
  1. $(1,2)$.
  2. $(2,1)$.
  3. $(2,0)$.
  4. $(3,0)$.
Explanation. $(2,1)$: $6 \le 12$, $5 \le 5$, value $7$. $(3,0)$ violates $2x_1 + x_2 \le 5$; $(1,2)$ is worth $5$; $(2,0)$ is worth $6$.
pli-f-462 · level facile · notes
The examples of the chapter on MIP heuristics (car, $P7$, $P42$, $P46$, $P72$) are maximization problems, while the definitions use $\min f(\vet x)$. How are they reconciled?
  1. The heuristics hold only for minimization problems.
  2. The direction of all constraints is reversed.
  3. Every variable $x_j$ is replaced by $-x_j$.
  4. A maximum of $\vet c\T \vet x$ is a minimum of $f(\vet x) = -\vet c\T \vet x$.
Explanation. The cutoff $f(\vet x) \le f(\bar{\vet x}) - \theta$ becomes $\vet c\T \vet x \ge \vet c\T \bar{\vet x} + \theta$.
pli-f-463 · level facile · notes
In the car example ($\max\ 3x_1 + 2x_2$, $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$, $4x_1 + 14x_2 \le 49$), the relaxation has optimum $(\frac72, \frac52)$. What does the nearest-integer rounding heuristic return?
  1. Failure: $(4,3)$ violates $10x_1 + 4x_2 \le 45$.
  2. The feasible solution $(4,3)$ of value $18$.
  3. The solution $(3,2)$ of value $13$.
  4. The optimal solution $(4,1)$.
Explanation. $\frac72$ and $\frac52$ round up to $4$ and $3$; the point is infeasible and rounding fails.
pli-f-464 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the rounding heuristic for a MIP is correct?
  1. It always returns a feasible solution, if the MIP is feasible.
  2. It always returns the optimal solution, if the relaxation has a unique optimum.
  3. It rounds to the nearest integer and may fail.
  4. It rounds all variables, including continuous ones.
Explanation. The rounded point may violate a constraint: in that case the heuristic declares failure.
pli-f-465 · level ipotesi · notes
In a maximization problem $\max\{\vet c\T\vet x : A\vet x \le b,\ \vet x \in \Z^n_{\ge 0}\}$, rounding down $\lfloor \tilde{\vet x} \rfloor$ an LP solution is always feasible if:
  1. all costs $c_j$ are positive.
  2. the relaxation has a unique optimal solution.
  3. the right-hand side $b$ is even.
  4. all coefficients $a_{ij}$ are nonnegative.
Explanation. Lowering the variables increases no left-hand side when $a_{ij} \ge 0$, and keeps $x \ge 0$.
pli-f-466 · level facile · notes
Why does rounding almost always fail on a MIP with equality constraints?
  1. Because the relaxation of an equality is infeasible.
  2. Because equalities make all variables integer.
  3. Independent roundings violate equalities.
  4. Because rounding does not apply to binary variables.
Explanation. For instance $x_1 + x_2 = 1$ with $(\frac12, \frac12)$ rounds to $(1,1)$; a diving is needed.
pli-f-467 · level facile · notes
What does diving do on a MIP?
  1. It fixes one variable at a time and re-solves the LP.
  2. It rounds all variables at once and stops.
  3. It explores the whole branch-and-bound tree.
  4. It searches the neighbourhood of the incumbent with a sub-MIP.
Explanation. It takes decisions one at a time, and after each one queries the relaxation again.
pli-f-468 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about diving for a MIP is correct?
  1. If the MIP is feasible, diving always finds a feasible solution.
  2. It is a single path in the branch-and-bound tree, without backtracking.
  3. Diving explores all children of every node.
  4. Diving always returns the optimal solution.
Explanation. If a fixing makes the LP infeasible, diving fails.
pli-f-469 · level facile · notes
In fractional diving on a MIP, which variable is fixed and to which value?
  1. The variable farthest from an integer, fixed downwards.
  2. The smallest-index variable, fixed to $0$.
  3. The closest to an integer, fixed to that integer.
  4. All fractional variables at the same time.
Explanation. Fixing the least fractional variable changes the LP solution little.
pli-f-470 · level facile · notes
What is the difference between rounding and diving in a MIP?
  1. Rounding decides everything from a single LP solution.
  2. Rounding recomputes the LP after each variable; diving does not.
  3. Diving does not use the LP relaxation.
  4. None: they are two names for the same heuristic.
Explanation. Recomputing the LP lets the solution adapt to the decisions taken.
pli-f-471 · level facile · notes
What is the purpose of the feasibility pump for a MIP?
  1. To find the optimal solution with few LPs.
  2. To improve an incumbent solution with a sub-MIP.
  3. To compute a dual bound on the optimal value.
  4. To find a feasible solution.
Explanation. For a general MIP even finding a feasible solution is hard.
pli-f-472 · level facile · notes
In the feasibility pump for a MIP, what alternates at each iteration?
  1. Adding a Gomory cut and solving the LP.
  2. Rounding and a minimum-distance LP.
  3. Branching on a variable and solving the children.
  4. Fixing a block of variables and solving a sub-MIP.
Explanation. The two points chase each other until they coincide: then the point is integer and satisfies the constraints.
pli-f-473 · level teorema · notes
In the feasibility pump, when the distance $\Delta(\vet x^*, \tilde{\vet x})$ between the LP point $\vet x^*$ and the integer point $\tilde{\vet x}$ becomes $0$, what can be concluded?

  2. $\vet x^*$ is feasible for the MIP.
  3. The MIP is infeasible.
  4. The integer point must be perturbed.
Explanation. Zero distance means the LP point coincides with an integer point on the integer variables.
pli-f-474 · level facile · notes
On $P72$, according to the chapter on MIP heuristics, the feasibility pump returns a solution of value $2$ while the optimum is $7$. Why?

  2. Because $P72$ has continuous variables.
  3. Because the pump rounds down.
  4. After the first LP the pump ignores the objective function.
Explanation. The quality of the solution is then improved with RINS, local branching or proximity search.
pli-f-475 · level facile · notes
What does the feasibility pump do when the rounded integer point repeats (a cycle)?

  2. It stops with the optimal solution.
  3. It adds a cover cut.
  4. It returns to the first LP with the original objective.
Explanation. Without perturbation the two points could alternate forever.
pli-f-476 · level facile · notes
Which common scheme do RINS, local branching and proximity search follow?

  2. They explore the whole branch-and-bound tree.
  3. They restrict the MIP around the incumbent.
  4. They fix all variables and solve an LP.
Explanation. It is the idea of large neighbourhood search: a large neighbourhood explored exactly.
pli-f-477 · level facile · notes
Consider a MIP $\min\{f(\vet x) : A\,\vet x \le b,\ \vet l \le \vet x \le \vet u,\ x_j \in \Z\ \forall j \in D\}$ with linear $f$ and $D$ the set of integer variables. With incumbent $\bar{\vet x} = (1, 0, 2, 3)$ and LP solution $\tilde{\vet x} = (1, \frac12, 2, \frac52)$, which variables does RINS fix?

  3. All, at the incumbent values.

pli-f-478 · level facile · notes
Consider a MIP $\min\{f(\vet x) : A\,\vet x \le b,\ \vet l \le \vet x \le \vet u,\ x_j \in \Z\ \forall j \in D\}$ with linear $f$ and $D$ the set of integer variables. In the RINS sub-MIP with incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$ and step $\theta > 0$, what does the cutoff constraint impose?
  1. $f(\vet x) \ge f(\bar{\vet x}) + \theta$.
  2. $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x}) \le \theta$.
  3. $f(\vet x) \le f(\bar{\vet x}) - \theta$.
  4. $f(\vet x) = f(\bar{\vet x})$.
Explanation. In a minimization problem, improving means lowering $f$ by at least $\theta$.
pli-f-479 · level facile · notes
What is the risk of the rigid fixing of variables in RINS?
  1. It may exclude the optimum from the sub-MIP.
  2. The sub-MIP becomes larger than the original MIP.
  3. RINS returns an infeasible solution.
  4. RINS never uses the LP solution.
Explanation. That is why local branching constrains how many variables change, not which.
pli-f-480 · level facile · notes
For a binary MIP with incumbent $\bar{\vet x} = (1, 0, 1, 0)$, what is the local branching constraint of radius $k = 1$?
  1. $x_1 + (1 - x_2) + x_3 + (1 - x_4) \le 1$.
  2. $(1 - x_1) + x_2 + (1 - x_3) + x_4 \le 1$.
  3. $(1 - x_1) + x_2 + (1 - x_3) + x_4 \ge 1$.
  4. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + x_4 \le 1$.
Explanation. $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x})$ adds $1 - x_j$ where $\bar x_j = 1$ and $x_j$ where $\bar x_j = 0$: it counts changed variables.
pli-f-481 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about local branching of radius $k$ around the incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$ of a binary MIP is correct?
  1. It fixes the variables on which incumbent and LP solution agree.
  2. It minimizes the distance from $\bar{\vet x}$ with a cutoff constraint.
  3. It decides how many variables can change.
  4. It requires exactly $k$ variables to change.
Explanation. The constraint $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x}) \le k$ is linear and keeps the original objective.
pli-f-482 · level facile · notes
For a binary MIP with $n$ variables, how many binary points does the local branching neighbourhood of radius $k = 2$ around $\bar{\vet x}$ contain?
  1. $1 + n + \binom{n}{2}$.
  2. $\binom{n}{2}$.
  3. $2^n$.
  4. $2n$.
Explanation. Points at distance $0$, $1$ and $2$: $\binom n0 + \binom n1 + \binom n2$.
pli-f-483 · level facile · notes
What does the proximity sub-MIP of proximity search around the incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$ minimize?
  1. The objective $f$, with the constraint $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x}) \le k$.
  2. The constraint violation of the rounded point.
  3. The number of fractional variables.
  4. The distance $\Delta(\vet x, \bar{\vet x})$, with the cutoff constraint $f(\vet x) \le f(\bar{\vet x}) - \theta$.
Explanation. It is the opposite of local branching: the cutoff is a constraint, the distance the objective.
pli-f-484 · level facile · notes
In proximity search, what is done when the proximity sub-MIP finds a solution?
  1. Stop: it is the optimal solution.
  2. The solution replaces the incumbent and a new sub-MIP around it is solved.
  3. Increase $\theta$ until the sub-MIP becomes infeasible, without changing the incumbent.
  4. Round the solution and restart from the relaxation.
Explanation. Repeat until the sub-MIP is infeasible (or a time limit).
pli-f-485 · level teorema · notes
In proximity search for a MIP with $f$ integer on all feasible solutions, what is concluded if the sub-MIP with $\theta = 1$ is infeasible?
  1. The MIP is infeasible.
  2. The incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$ is optimal.
  3. The radius $k$ must be increased.
  4. The incumbent is not feasible.
Explanation. No solution has $f(\vet x) \le f(\bar{\vet x}) - 1$; with integer $f$ none has $f(\vet x) < f(\bar{\vet x})$.
pli-f-486 · level facile · notes
On which structure of MIPs are relax-and-fix and fix-and-optimize based?
  1. All variables continuous.
  2. All constraints equalities.
  3. A totally unimodular constraint matrix.
  4. Integer variables in ordered blocks.
Explanation. A sequence of small MIPs is solved instead of a large one.
pli-f-487 · level facile · notes
In relax-and-fix with blocks $B_1, \dots, B_p$, how are the variables treated at step $r$?

  2. All integer, and the full MIP is solved.
  3. $B_r$ relaxed, all others integer.
  4. $B_r$ fixed at the incumbent, the others integer.
Explanation. Relaxing the future blocks estimates the effect of the decisions not yet taken.
pli-f-488 · level ipotesi · notes
Relax-and-fix can fail even if the MIP is feasible. Which feature of the method explains it?

  2. The future blocks are fixed to zero.
  3. Decisions taken are never revised.
  4. The method uses a cutoff constraint.
Explanation. Like diving, it is a method without backtracking.
pli-f-489 · level facile · notes
What does fix-and-optimize do starting from an incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$ and windows $W_1, \dots, W_q$?

  2. It builds a solution block by block without an incumbent.
  3. It rounds the LP solution window by window.
  4. It re-optimizes one window at a time, rest fixed.
Explanation. A better solution becomes the incumbent; passes are repeated until no improvement.
pli-f-490 · level facile · notes

  1. After the first window.
  2. When it finds a feasible solution.
  3. After a pass with no improvement.
  4. When the relaxation becomes integer.
Explanation. Each pass may improve the incumbent; without improvement it is a local optimum with respect to the windows.
pli-f-491 · level facile · notes
What is the difference between relax-and-fix and fix-and-optimize?

  3. None: they are the same heuristic.
  4. Fix-and-optimize does not use the MIP solver.
Explanation. The first is constructive, the second an improvement method.
pli-f-492 · level ipotesi · notes

  4. That every sub-MIP has a single solution.
Explanation. The incumbent remains a fallback solution at every step.

pli-f-494 · level facile · notes

pli-f-546 · level facile · notes

pli-f-547 · level facile · notes

pli-f-548 · level facile · notes

  1. It sets the radius of the neighbourhood.
  2. It sets how many variables may change.
  3. The minimum improvement required.
  4. It is the integrality tolerance.

pli-f-549 · level facile · notes
In local branching, what is done if the sub-MIP with radius $k$ finds no solution better than the incumbent?

  3. Round the LP solution.
  4. The radius $k$ can be enlarged.
Explanation. The absence of improvements in the neighbourhood does not prove global optimality.

  2. $15$
  3. $9$
  4. $8$

  1. $\max\ -4\,x_1 - 5\,x_2$, $5\,x_1 + 6\,x_2 \le 5$, $5\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 29$
  2. $\max\ 4\,x_1 + 5\,x_2$, $-5\,x_1 - 6\,x_2 \le -5$, $5\,x_1 + 4\,x_2 \le 29$

In the ILP problem $\max\ 3\,x_1 + 5\,x_2$ subject to $9\,x_1 + 6\,x_2 \le 42$, $6\,x_1 + 12\,x_2 \le 35$, $x_1, x_2 \in \Z_{\ge 0}$ the LP relaxation has the optimal solution $\tilde{\vet x}(\LP) = (\frac{49}{12}, \frac{7}{8})$ of value $z(\LP) = \frac{133}{8}$. Rounding down gives the feasible solution $(4, 0)$. What is the tightest interval for the optimal value $z(\ILP)$ of the ILP problem certified by these data?
  1. $12 \le z(\ILP) \le 16$
  2. $12 \le z(\ILP) \le 17$
  3. $12 \le z(\ILP) \le \frac{133}{8}$
  4. $z(\ILP) = 12$
Explanation. The rounded solution gives the lower bound $12$; variables and costs are integer, so $z(\ILP) \le \lfloor z(\LP) \rfloor = 16$. Rounding up is not valid, and the rounded solution is not certified optimal.
